Chapter 1
The academic years 1976–77 and
1977–78
Scope and chronology. This chapter audits all 23 requested catalogue records and
all 55 supplied scan pages.
The mathematical core belongs to 1976–77.
One re-
quested record does not: P000753 dates record 437 to 13 February 1978, not the
catalogue’s 13 February 1977. It is retained as a chronological appendix, with cor-
rected date and 1977-437 occurrence identifiers. The five adjacent pages P000705–
P000706, P000752 and P000757–P000758 are accounted for without assigning their
new content to 1976–77. Plans and year headings remain visible records. The archive
list is a finding aid, not the authority for a conflicting manuscript date (Moscow Center
for Continuous Mathematical Education, 2026).
The year’s lines. The opening talk connects real integral geometry on SL(2, R)
with holomorphic domains and separation of discrete series. Its strongest exact match
is the 1977 Gelfand–Gindikin paper; the notebook’s projector kernel omits a noncon-
stant trace factor (Gel’fand and Gindikin, 1977, Eq. 22 and Proposition 9).
Fuchs
and Kirillov revisit the Weil algebra and transgression. Arnold’s discussion of second-
order equations is principally classical projective geometry: point linearization re-
quires the full Lie–Tresse conditions, not a vanishing coefficient along one solution
(Cartan, 1924, §§VII–VIII).
Superalgebras, the Wess–Zumino multiplet, Berezin integration and Stokes bound-
aries continue the preceding year’s supergeometry.
The distinction between an
ordinary differential form and an integrable superdensity is essential. The Bernstein–
Leites boundary theorem is a precise later publication, while the Popov–Daikhin
Einstein–Yang–Mills construction had already appeared in 1975 (Bernstein and
Leites, 1977; Popov and Daikhin, 1975). The latter match exposes a speaker-initial
conflict that the notes alone cannot resolve.
Migdal’s confinement discussion re-
mains a physical programme; its class E does not assert a theorem of continuum
confinement.
The winter material links Euler products and affine Lie algebras with integrable
equations. Manin’s filtered bimodules, spectral curves and nodal gluing have exact
counterparts in his 1978 survey, translated in 1979. Comparing formulas, rather than
titles, also identifies an erroneous flatness sign shared by the notebook and the printed
survey (Manin, 1979, Ch. III, Theorem 2.6). Duistermaat’s variational index formula
had circulated in May 1974; his spectral discussion leads to the later joint paper with
Kolk and Varadarajan. Gabrielov’s hypersimplicial chains and Graev’s admissible line
complexes likewise belong to established programmes with earlier publications, not
newly discovered orphan problems.
The spring notes connect Poncelet closure, almost Euclidean sections, Young di-
agrams and representation packets.
Vershik’s limit-shape announcement is distin-

guished from the later Gaussian fluctuation theorem: the latter is a generalized ran-
dom field with a specified covariance, not the notebook’s unqualified white noise
(Ivanov and Olshanski, 2002, §7). Tyurin’s universal assertion that varying the com-
plex structure can merge any two irreducible representations fails already for distinct
unitary characters; his own later survey records the relevant uniqueness principle
(Tyurin, 1978, Ch. II, §2, Exercise 10). The 1978 appendix records the BGG corre-
spondence and distinguishes it from Beilinson’s related construction (Bernstein et al.,
1978; Beilinson, 1978).

Questions and limits. The problem index contains 15 definite questions, includ-
ing resolved questions and explicitly linked earlier occurrences. No item meets the
C/D development gate. Unspecified range spaces, continuous-order matrices, deleted
global conditions and unqualified behaviour questions remain F; they supply no evi-
dence of an undeveloped theorem. The development section contains complete crit-
ical checks and counterexamples, marked as our verifications, with no claim of re-
search novelty. Historical fate, literal correctness and depth of verification are sepa-
rate assessments throughout.
Continuities.
The supplied earlier index provides particularly useful routes
through 1975 records 366 and 370 (Euler products), 374–385 and 391 (spectral
curves and inverse constructions), 377 and 386 (gauge theory), and 389 (superge-
ometry). Older Morse/Maslov and integral-geometric threads provide context. The
BGG question over projective space is not merged with the earlier affine Serre–
Quillen–Suslin problem. Related vocabulary is never enough to establish identity of
questions.
1.1
I. M. Gelfand, 1976-09-06: A physics reading
programme
record 397; source pages P000659.
The opening question is Что надо знать из физики?; an uncertain marginal attri-
bution says “following Sinai?”. The programme lists classical and Hamiltonian me-
chanics; quantum mechanics and scattering; relativity and field theory; and quantum
field theory, including second quantization, Bose and Fermi statistics, functional inte-
grals, perturbation series, Feynman diagrams, renormalization and Yang–Mills fields.
Arnold, Landau–Lifshitz, Fermi, Weinberg and Hepp are reading suggestions. Consul-
tation with physicists and a typed version are requested. No theorem, calculation or
definite research problem is stated, so the programme is preserved without manufac-
turing mathematical occurrences. Gindikin begins on the same page; the catalogue’s
P000659–P000660 window overlaps that next record.

1.2
S. G. Gindikin, with I. M. Gelfand, 1976-09-06: SL(2,
R), integral geometry and analytic separation of
series
record 398; source pages P000659-P000662.
The group is represented as the determinant-one quadric, and the regular repre-
sentation is studied through hyperplane and line integrals. Gelfand distinguishes sep-
aration into series from decomposition into individual irreducibles. The announced
real-transform kernels isolate discrete and continuous parts; two complex domains
then realize the positive and negative discrete parts as Hardy boundary values. The
lecture ends with an explicit kernel and Arnold’s conjecture identifying the domains
by their fractional-linear action. The exact subsequent paper is Gel’fand and Gindikin
(1977), received 6 June 1977. Its kernel has an additional nonconstant factor absent
from the notebook. P000661–P000662 unmistakably continue this lecture, despite
the catalogue’s narrower page window.
1976-398.1
The determinant-one quadric of signature (2,2)
Class E
Kind; source. construction; P000659
The notes. For g =

α β
γ δ

, the notes identify SL(2, R) with αδ −βγ = 1, then with
x2 + y2 −z2 −t2 = 1.
Statement. The linear substitution x = (α+δ)/2, z = (α−δ)/2, y = (β−γ)/2, t = (β+γ)/2
is invertible and satisfies det g = x2 + y2 −z2 −t2.
Thus SL(2, R) is precisely the
nonsingular real level set of this quadratic form.
Reconstruction. [proved here] The explicit change of coordinates is supplied here;
expanding the four squares verifies the identification.
Fate. This is the classical quadric model used again in Gindikin (2000, pp.417–420);
it is background, not a 1976 novelty.
Present status. As of 10 November 2026: The identification is exact and elementary;
no open question is attached to it.
Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: exact; verification: read
the result; development: none; confidence: high
1976-398.2
Which linear symmetries come from left and right
translations?
Class E
Kind; source. claim; P000659
The notes. The formula is g 7→g−1
1 gg2. The next sentence says that any linear trans-
formation preserving the hyperboloid has this form.
Statement. For a, b ∈SL(2, R), X 7→aXb−1 gives a homomorphism onto SO0(2, 2), with
kernel {(I, I), (−I, −I)}. It does not exhaust O(2, 2). For example X 7→XT preserves
det X but has determinant −1 as a linear map on the four-dimensional matrix space.
Reconstruction. [proved here] The transpose counterexample checks the literal
quantifier. The notebook’s inverse placement defines a right action on points; pull-
back Tg1,g2f(X) = f(g−1
1 Xg2) is a representation on functions.
These conventions
should not be conflated.
Fate. The left–right symmetry is standard in the real-quadric construction, as in
Gindikin (2000, pp.417–419). The omitted identity-component restriction is a note-
book error, not later progress.
Present status. As of 10 November 2026: The corrected identity-component state-
ment holds. The unrestricted statement remains false.
Assessment. literal statement: false as written; publication match: partial; verifi-
cation: read the result; development: none; confidence: high

1976-398.3
Stabilizers of points of the quadric
Class E
Kind; source. problem; P000659-P000660
The notes. The stabilizer of the identity is the diagonal subgroup, followed by the
exercise “compute the stabilizer subgroups of other points”.
Statement. For x ∈SL(2, R), the pairs satisfying g−1
1 xg2 = x are exactly {(xhx−1, h) : h ∈
SL(2, R)}. Hence every stabilizer is conjugate to the diagonal and G ≃(G×G)/ diag G,
with the action convention understood.
Reconstruction. [proved here] Multiply the fixed-point equation by g1 on the left and
by x−1 on the right. This solves the exercise completely.
Fate. This is the elementary homogeneous-space structure underlying Gindikin
(2000, opening construction); no independent research problem is created for the
exercise.
Present status. As of 10 November 2026: Completely resolved by the displayed
algebraic calculation.
Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: exact; verification: read
the result; development: none; confidence: high
1976-398.4
Haar measure and the Hilbert space of the regular
representation
Class E
Kind; source. construction; P000660
The notes. On β ̸= 0, coordinates are (α, δ, β), and dg = dα dβ dδ/|β|. The space is
H = L2(G, dg).
Statement. The measure δ(αδ−βγ−1) dα dβ dγ dδ is invariant under both determinant-
one left and right multiplications. Integrating out γ = (αδ−1)/β gives the stated local
Haar density, up to an overall positive constant. The left–right pullbacks are unitary
on L2(G).
Reconstruction. [proved here] The delta-function calculation is the coarea identity
on the chart β ̸= 0; it does not claim that this single chart covers all of G.
Fate. The same invariant quadric measure is used in Gindikin (2000, real geometry,
pp.421–423).
Present status. As of 10 November 2026: Standard Haar measure; the chart re-
striction and normalization are essential but routine.
Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: exact; verification: read
the result; development: none; confidence: high
1976-398.5
Separating series before decomposing into
irreducibles
Class E
Kind; source. problem; P000660
The notes. The word “irreducible” is crossed out. Gelfand calls division into series
algebraic and division of a series into representations transcendental; the example
is explicitly said to be well known.
Statement. For the left–right regular representation of SL(2, R), seek invariant
orthogonal projectors onto the closed sum L2
disc(G) of square-integrable discrete-
series matrix-coefficient spaces and its continuous-spectrum orthogonal comple-
ment, and then onto the holomorphic and antiholomorphic discrete parts. The first
question is coarser than specifying the Plancherel measure and every irreducible
constituent.
Reconstruction. The words “algebraic” and “transcendental” are a research prefer-
ence, not a proved dichotomy. We formulate the definite projector problem using
closed Hilbert subspaces.
Fate. The spectral decomposition predates the talk: Strichartz (1973, entry R18(a))
records the 1973 hyperboloid analysis.
The specifically complex-analytic imple-

receive class A.
Present status. As of 10 November 2026: The separation problem for this group is
solved. Different analytic realizations must be distinguished from the older exis-
tence of the Plancherel decomposition.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:

1976-398.6
Classification of real hyperplane sections
Class E
Kind; source. construction; P000660-P000661
The notes. For ξα + ηδ −pβ −qγ = ζ, the notes classify sections using ∆= ξη −pq.
The degenerate case mixes “hyperbolic paraboloid”, “hyperbolic cylinder” and a
two-parameter label; P000661 gives paraboloids three parameters.
Statement. Assume the normal covector is nonzero and identify proportional hy-
perplane equations. If ∆< 0, the section is a one-sheeted hyperboloid. If ∆> 0,
it is one-sheeted, two-sheeted or a cone according as |ζ| is greater than, less than
or equal to 2
√
∆. If ∆= 0, ζ ̸= 0 gives a hyperbolic paraboloid (three parameters),
whereas ζ = 0 gives a hyperbolic cylinder (two parameters). General nondegener-
ate sections have four parameters; tangent cones have three.
Reconstruction. [proved here] For the null normal, reduce to α = ζ. When ζ ̸= 0,
δ = (1 + βγ)/ζ; when ζ = 0, βγ = −1 and δ is free. This independently resolves the
conflated degenerate cases.
Fate. The section and horosphere geometry is treated in Gindikin (2000, pp.418–
423); the underlying quadratic classification is elementary and predates the lecture.
Present status. As of 10 November 2026: Resolved with the null-level distinction.
The two- and three-parameter families are different, not conflicting counts for one
family.
Assessment. literal statement: false as written; publication match: partial; verifi-
cation: read the result; development: none; confidence: high
1976-398.7
The hyperplane Radon transform on the quadric
Class E
Kind; source. construction; P000661
The notes. The transform is bψ =
R
G ψ(g)δ(ξα+ηδ−pβ−qγ−ζ) dg, with ψ ∈L2(G) written
without a domain qualification. The families A, B, D, E are then named.
Statement. For ψ ∈C∞
c (G), the displayed expression defines a distribution in the hy-
perplane parameters by pushforward; at transverse sections it is the usual surface
integral with the coarea density. It is homogeneous of degree −1 under simultane-
ous nonzero real scaling of the five parameters, with absolute value in the scaling
factor. For general L2 input, pointwise surface integration requires a specified ex-
tension or regularization.
Reconstruction. The test-function domain and distributional interpretation are sup-
plied to avoid assigning a trace on a measure-zero surface to an arbitrary L2 equiv-
alence class.
Fate. Compare the precise transform domains in Gindikin (2000, complex and real
geometry). The definition is classical; its unrestricted L2 pointwise reading is not.
Present status. As of 10 November 2026: Well defined on test functions and in
appropriate distributional extensions. The notebook does not specify one extension
on all of L2(G).
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
partial; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high

1976-398.8
The unspecified range of the four- and
three-parameter transforms
Class F
Kind; source. question; P000661
The notes. The question is Как описать, какие ф-и 4-х и 3-х перем. появляются?
No support, decay, regularity or boundary conditions are attached.
Statement. A range problem would have to specify a source space X(G), one of
the section families F, its density normalization, and a target space Y (F), then
characterize RF(X). These choices are missing, so there is no uniquely determined
proposition here.
Reconstruction. We do not replace this question by a familiar Euclidean range the-
orem, or identify inversion with surjectivity onto a characterized range.
Fate. Targeted searches located Gindikin (2000, main inversion construction), but
that does not identify the omitted function-space quantifiers. The 1977 Hardy the-
orem concerns a different, specified boundary-value problem.
Present status. As of 10 November 2026: Unidentified at the exact range-theorem
level. No assertion that it remains open, and no new problem-index entry, is justi-
fied.
Assessment. literal statement: not determinable; publication match: thematic only;
verification: unresolved lead; development: none; confidence: low
1976-398.9
Orthogonal discrete and continuous parts
Class E
Kind; source. theorem; P000661
The notes. “Main theorem 1” begins H = HA
◦+ HB; the spaces were introduced as
kernels of the two kinds of hyperplane integration.
Statement. The unambiguous spectral statement is L2(G) = L2
disc(G) ⊕L2
cont(G), an
orthogonal decomposition invariant under both translations, where the first sum-
mand is the closed span of discrete-series matrix coefficients and the second its
orthogonal complement. The identification of these summands with the notebook’s
integral kernels additionally requires precise domains for those transforms.
Reconstruction. The spectral statement is separated from the more delicate ker-
nel realization in the next occurrence. The circle over the plus sign is read as an
orthogonal direct sum, consistently with the later projector discussion.
Fate. The existence of the spectral decomposition precedes 1976; see the 1973
paper recorded in Strichartz (1973, entry R18(a)). The exact kernel formulations
were not checked in that inaccessible article.
Present status. As of 10 November 2026: The spectral decomposition is established.
Verification of this occurrence is indirect for the prior publication, not a claim to
have read Strichartz’s theorem.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
partial; verification: indirect; development: none; confidence: medium

Reconstruction. The modern cuspidal interpretation supplies a well-defined part of

1976-398.11
An ultrahyperbolic equation for the transform
Class E
Kind; source. claim; P000661
The notes. The transformed function is said to satisfy a second-order ultrahyperbolic
equation “that can be written down”, but no operator is displayed.
Statement. For the test-function transform above, in the sense of distributions,
(∂ξ∂η −∂p∂q −∂2
ζ) bψ = 0.
Indeed differentiation under the integral inserts αδ −βγ −1 = 0. Additional range
conditions do not follow merely from this equation.
Reconstruction. [proved here] The explicit PDE is reconstructed directly from the
determinant equation; it is not a formula actually written in the notebook. The two
minus signs in the p, q coefficients cancel in the mixed derivative.
Fate. This is the classical differential constraint of a Radon transform supported
on a quadric; compare the transform setting in Gindikin (2000, pp.418–423). The
derivation here checks only the asserted necessary PDE.
Present status. As of 10 November 2026: The reconstructed necessary condition is
proved directly. It is not a sufficient range characterization.
Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: partial; verification:

1976-398.12
The unexplained minimality of the families A, D and
E
Class F
Kind; source. claim; P000661
The notes. A separate sentence says Сем-ва A, D, E явл. минимальными.
Statement. No order on families, admissible restriction, local completeness notion
or minimal parameter count is supplied. Consequently “minimal” cannot be con-
verted into a uniquely testable theorem from this sentence alone.
Reconstruction. Minimality under inclusion, minimal dimension for inversion, and
minimality for detecting the continuous series are different assertions; none is se-
lected editorially.
Fate. The exact-phrase and horospherical searches led to Gindikin (2000), but no
precise claim matching this unqualified sentence was identified.
Present status. As of 10 November 2026: Unidentified, rather than certified open

1976-398.13
Straight rulings as translates of a unipotent
subgroup
Class E
Kind; source. construction; P000661
The notes. The examples are ( 1 z
0 1 ),
  h
z
0 h−1

, and the general left–right translate; the
ruling family has three parameters.
Statement. Every affine real line contained in det X = 1 is A(I + tN), where A ∈
SL(2, R), N ̸= 0, tr N = det N = 0. Thus N2 = 0, and up to reparametrization the line
is a left–right translate of the upper unipotent subgroup. The space of unoriented
lines has dimension three.
Reconstruction. [proved here] Expand det(A + tB) = 1 + t tr(A−1B) + t2 det(A−1B). At
each A, null directions form a one-dimensional projective cone; subtracting the one
line parameter from 3 + 1 gives dimension 3.
Fate. These are the real horocycles in Gindikin (2000, pp.418–422); the classifica-
tion follows by the elementary determinant calculation.
Present status. As of 10 November 2026: The construction and count are exact,
with zero direction excluded.
Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: exact; verification: read
the result; development: none; confidence: high
1976-398.14
The two complex domains and the Siegel coordinate
chart
Class A
Kind; source. construction; P000661-P000662
The notes. On the complex determinant-one quadric the inequality is ℑ(α¯β)ℑ(δ ¯β) >
(ℑβ)2. The two signs are said to give domains with distinguished boundary G; in
three coordinates the boundary is called R3.
Statement. On β ̸= 0, put Z =

α/β −1/β
−1/β
δ/β

. The inequality is equivalent to det ℑZ > 0,
hence ℑZ is positive or negative definite. These are the two sign components in this
chart. Their invariant continuation in SL(2, C) has the real group as its distinguished
boundary in the relevant Hardy realization. The chart omits Z12 = 0; neither this
parametrization nor the phrase R3 identifies the entire group with one Euclidean
chart.
Reconstruction. [proved here] The coordinate calculation multiplies det ℑZ by |β|4.
“Distinguished boundary” requires a specified holomorphic function algebra or
Hardy norm; it is not defined for arbitrary unbounded holomorphic functions solely
by uniqueness.
Fate. Gel’fand and Gindikin (1977, §3, Proposition 5) publishes the same inequality
and domain construction in 1977, after the 1976 talk.
Present status. As of 10 November 2026: The invariant domain realization is es-
tablished; the global chart and function-class qualifications must accompany the
notebook shorthand.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
partial; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high; problem

1976-398.15
A distinguished boundary is more than a
uniqueness set
Class E
Kind; source. definition; P000662
The notes. The parenthetical definition calls the skeleton the smallest boundary set
from which a holomorphic function continuous on the closure is uniquely recovered.
Statement. For a unital uniform algebra A ⊂C(K) on a compact Hausdorff space,
the Shilov boundary is the smallest closed set S ⊂K such that ∥f∥K = supS |f| for


===== PDF PAGE 9 =====
CHAPTER 1. THE ACADEMIC YEARS 1976–77 AND 1977–78
9
every f ∈A. Mere uniqueness is weaker: for the disc algebra, every nontrivial
closed boundary arc is a uniqueness set, whereas the Shilov boundary is the whole
circle. Two disjoint such arcs show that a smallest uniqueness set need not exist.
Reconstruction. [proved here] Boundary vanishing on an arc gives analytic contin-
uation by Schwarz reflection and then the identity theorem. Conversely, (1 + ¯ζz)/2
peaks at any prescribed unit-circle point ζ, forcing every closed norming boundary
to contain the whole circle. For the unbounded seminar domains one must specify
the function class or compactification.
Fate. The classical minimal closed norming-boundary definition is recalled precisely
in Acosta (2006, §1). The notebook’s uniqueness-only definition is not equivalent to
it.
Present status. As of 10 November 2026: The standard definition is established; the
literal uniqueness-only definition is false in this generality.
Assessment. literal statement: false as written; publication match: partial; verifi-
cation: read the result; development: annotated; confidence: high
1976-398.16
Hardy boundary values separate the two discrete
series
Class A
Kind; source. theorem; P000662
The notes. “Main theorem 2” says ψ ∈HA iff ψ = ψ+ + ψ−, where the two functions
continue analytically to D+, D−. It also asserts zero intersection because everything
is in L2.
Statement. Let H2(D±) be the invariant Hardy spaces with uniformly bounded L2(G)
norms on interior translates approaching the real distinguished boundary. Their
boundary maps are isometries onto the holomorphic and antiholomorphic discrete-
series summands H±. These summands are orthogonal, H+ ∩H−= 0, and L2
disc(G) =
H+ ⊕H−. Mere holomorphic continuation, without the Hardy bound and the speci-
fied boundary-value sense, is insufficient as a definition.
Reconstruction. The Hardy condition and boundary-limit interpretation are restored
from the published theorem; they are not written in the notebook.
Fate. The precise statement appears in Gel’fand and Gindikin (1977, Theorem 3,
Proposition 4 and §4), received 6 June 1977.
Present status. As of 10 November 2026:
Established for the specified Hardy
spaces. The later continuous-series cohomological realization should not be sub-
stituted for this discrete-series theorem.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
exact; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high; problem
GP-1976-01

The holomorphic projector kernel and its missing

series projector, write t = tr(g1g−1
2 ) = λ + λ−1, choosing the holomorphic eigenvalue
branch |λ| > 1 in the relevant semigroup. Up to a nonzero normalization constant
and its consistent branch, the kernel is

Its distributional boundary value gives the orthogonal projector. The extra factor
(t + 2)−1/2 cannot be absorbed in c.

1976-398.18
The Fourier-integral technique behind the projector
Class A
Kind; source. method; P000662
The notes. The concluding heading calls the “submerged part” Fourier-integral
technique and describes K as an analogue of the Cauchy kernel.
Statement. In the verified construction, discrete-series characters are analytically
continued into the semigroup and summed with their Plancherel weights to obtain
a holomorphic reproducing kernel; its boundary value acts as a projection on L2(G).
This is a concrete implementation of the Cauchy-kernel analogy, not a separate
general theorem about Fourier integral operators.
Reconstruction. The character-series procedure is supplied by the publication. The
notebook itself records only the method label, with no phase, amplitude or symbol
calculus.
Fate. Gel’fand and Gindikin (1977, §4, equations 15–22) supplies the precise con-
struction in 1977.
Present status. As of 10 November 2026: The bounded method claim is identified.
No broader Fourier-integral-operator theorem is attributed to this one sentence.
Assessment. literal statement: not determinable; publication match: partial; verifi-
cation: read the result; development: none; confidence: high
1976-398.19
Arnold’s Möbius characterization of the two
domains
Class A
Kind; source. conjecture; P000662; V. I. Arnold
The notes. Arnold conjectures that D+ consists of transformations taking the disc
into itself and D−of transformations taking its exterior into itself. The last line says
SL(2, R) preserves the circle.
Statement. After Cayley conjugation from the real-projective-line/upper-half-plane
model to the unit-circle model, the relevant open domains are the strict Möbius
contraction semigroups: one sends the closed unit disc into the open disc, and the
other sends the closed exterior in CP1 into the open exterior. Their real edge is
the circle-preserving form conjugate to SL(2, R), modulo its central kernel. Without
conjugation, the standard real matrices preserve RP1, not the standard unit circle.
Reconstruction. The words “strict”, the compactified exterior and the Cayley
change of real form are required to distinguish the open domains from the semi-
group boundary.
Fate. Gel’fand and Gindikin (1977, §3, Proposition 9) proves precisely this geometric
identification in 1977.

Academic-year heading: 1976–77
record 399; source pages P000659-P000664.
This catalogue entry labels the academic year and overlaps the opening lectures. It
supplies no separately identifiable mathematical exposition. The actual occurrences

1.4
A. A. Kirillov, 1976-09-13: Superalgebras,
supergroups and supermanifolds: announced
heading
record 400; source pages P000663.
Immediately below the date 13/IX-76, the manuscript gives Kirillov’s name and this
title, then explicitly switches to Fuchs and the Weil algebra. No intervening exposition
is recorded.
The title is retained as a programme heading; Fuchs’s formulas and
Kirillov’s later intervention are assigned to records 401 and 402. The broad catalogue
window P000662–P000664 must not be read as an independent three-page Kirillov

1.5
D. B. Fuchs, 1976-09-13: The Weil algebra and its
differential
record 401; source pages P000663-P000664.
Fuchs constructs the graded algebra Λg∗⊗Sg∗, specifies its differential on gener-
ators, explains the signed Leibniz rule and gives the structure-constant formula on
the symmetric generators. The concluding acyclicity statement omits the surviving
degree-zero constants. Characteristic classes are announced for a future meeting, not
developed here. This is established Weil–Cartan theory: Cartan (1950b,a). The lec-
ture begins on P000663 and ends before the explicit Kirillov intervention on P000664.
1976-401.1
The graded Weil algebra
Class E
Kind; source. definition; P000663
The notes. W(g) = Λ∗(g′) ⊗S∗(g′), viewed as functions of 2n variables, with Λp ⊗Sq in
degree p + 2q.
Statement. For a finite-dimensional Lie algebra g over R or C, W(g) is the free
graded-commutative algebra on odd generators θi of cohomological degree 1 and
even generators ui of degree 2, two copies of g∗. Thus its underlying superdimen-
sion is n|n; “functions” here means polynomials in the even variables and exterior
polynomials in the odd ones.
Reconstruction. The field, finite-dimensionality and polynomial interpretation are
supplied. No smooth or completed function algebra is implied.

1976-401.3
The unique Weil differential on both sets of
generators
Class E
Kind; source. construction; P000663-P000664
The notes. The differential sends h⊗1 to (d+h)⊗1+1⊗h, is a graded derivation, and
is said to extend uniquely with square zero. P000664 gives d(1 ⊗hk) = ck

Statement. If [ei, ej] = ck

dθk = 1

1.6
A. A. Kirillov, 1976-09-13: Transgression and
invariant forms on compact Lie groups
record 402; source pages P000664.
A short intervention explains the purpose of the Weil algebra in computing compact
Lie-group cohomology. The intended correspondence is between primitive odd coho-
mology generators and indecomposable symmetric invariant polynomials. The note-
book states a stronger, false dimension equality for all invariant forms and reverses
the differential direction in its transgression shorthand. The surrounding Fuchs and
Arnold lectures belong to separate records.
1976-402.1
Computing compact Lie-group cohomology from
invariant forms
Class E
Kind; source. method; P000664
The notes. Kirillov says that the algebra was devised for computing the homologies
of simple compact Lie groups, and that invariant symmetric forms are easier to find
than skew-symmetric ones.
Statement. For a compact connected Lie group K over R, averaging identifies de
Rham cohomology with the cohomology of invariant forms; bi-invariant forms give
the harmonic representatives for a bi-invariant metric. The cohomology algebra is
exterior on primitive odd-degree generators. Invariant polynomials (Sk∗)K, together
with transgression, determine their degrees. Simplicity is not needed for this real-
coefficient statement.
Reconstruction. Connectedness, compactness and real coefficients are explicit.
This does not assert an integral-cohomology computation, where torsion is invisi-
ble to differential forms.
Fate. Cartan (1950a, Exposé 20, §§3–4) supplies the transgression method before
1976.
Present status. As of 10 November 2026: Established real-coefficient theory. The
method statement is distinct from the incorrect unrestricted counting rule in the
next occurrence.


===== PDF PAGE 14 =====
CHAPTER 1. THE ACADEMIC YEARS 1976–77 AND 1977–78
14
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
exact; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high
1976-402.2
Primitive odd forms versus indecomposable
symmetric invariants
Class E
Kind; source. claim; P000664
The notes. The conclusion reads: as many invariant skew-symmetric forms of de-
gree k as symmetric ones of degree (k + 1)/2. A preceding sentence calls an odd
form the differential of a symmetric expression.
Statement. Let I = (Sk∗)K, I+ its positive-degree ideal, and Q(I) = I+/(I+)2. For
compact connected K, transgression identifies Q(I)m with the primitive part of
H2m−1(K; R), up to the usual choice of generators. A degree-m invariant polyno-
mial has Weil degree 2m and is d of a transgression element of degree 2m−1, whose
exterior component represents the corresponding primitive class. The differential
direction in the notebook must therefore be reversed.
Reconstruction. [proved here] For K = SU(2), the square of the Killing quadratic
polynomial is a nonzero invariant symmetric polynomial of degree 4, whereas
Λ7su(2)∗= 0. This disproves the literal count at k = 7, while its indecomposable
version is correct.
Fate. The corrected generator correspondence is Cartan (1950a, Exposé 20, §4,
Theorem 2).
Present status. As of 10 November 2026: Established for primitive and indecompos-
able generators. The unrestricted equality and the displayed differential direction
are false as written.
Assessment. literal statement: false as written; publication match: partial; verifi-
cation: read the result; development: annotated; confidence: high
1.7
V. I. Arnold, 1976-09-13: Point equivalence of
second-order equations and projective geometry
record 403; source pages P000664-P000666.
Arnold explicitly revisits nineteenth-century work of Lie, Tresse and Liouville and
Cartan’s 1924 projective connections. The lecture separates point linearization, pairs
of line fields in a contact three-manifold, projective parameters along solutions, and
inequivalent projective structures on a circle. It then normalizes the quadratic trans-
verse terms, obtains a relative 5/2-density, and constructs projective scalar invariants
from its reciprocal power. A damaged global question and Gelfand’s proposed coho-
mological connection are recorded without inventing missing quantifiers. The local
normalization calculations can be checked, but the stated flatness implication needs
additional hypotheses.
1976-403.1
When can a scalar second-order ODE be straightened
by point transformations?
Class E
Kind; source. question; P000664
The notes. For y′′ = F(x, y, y′), Arnold asks whether solution curves can be straight-
ened. The page explicitly cites Tresse 1896 and Acta Mathematica 1894, and con-
trasts general equations with y′′ = 0.
Statement. For a smooth scalar equation on an open set in (x, y, p), local equivalence
under a nonsingular point change (x, y) 7→(X, Y ), on a chart where dX/dx ̸= 0, to
YXX = 0 holds exactly when the two relative invariants vanish identically there:
I = Fpppp,
H = D2Fpp −4DFyp −FpDFpp + 6Fyy −3FyFpp + 4FpFyp,
D = ∂x + p∂y + F∂p.

Present status. As of 10 November 2026: The local point-linearization problem is

1976-403.2
Every linear second-order equation is locally
point-flat
Class E
Kind; source. theorem; P000664
The notes. The notes state that y′′ + a(x)y′ + b(x)y = 0 becomes y′′ = 0 after a change
of independent variable and a change linear in y; configuration theorems such as
Desargues are then transferred to the solution family.
Statement. Let a, b be smooth on an interval and let u, v be independent solutions,
with v ̸= 0 on a smaller interval. Then X = u/v, Y = y/v is a local point diffeomor-
phism because X′ = W/v2 ̸= 0, and every solution becomes Y = c1X + c2. Any in-
cidence theorem formulated for straight-line configurations transfers to configura-

zeros or monodromy is asserted.
Reconstruction. [proved here] The ratio-of-solutions proof also states the actual do-
main restriction. The doubtful Russian phrase translated “linear menu” is not used
to infer extra content.
Fate. This classical fact is recalled in Kruglikov (2009, introduction); Arnold
presents it explicitly as old material.
Present status. As of 10 November 2026:
Completely established locally.
The
incidence-theorem remark is a consequence of transporting points and curves by
this diffeomorphism.

1976-403.3
Cartan geometry of a general second-order equation
Class E
Kind; source. construction; P000665
The notes. Cartan 1924–25 is invoked: solutions are geodesics of a projective con-
nection. The next lines pass to the space of line elements (x, y, y′).
Statement. A regular second-order equation defines a path geometry on the three-
dimensional contact-element space and has a canonical normal Cartan connection
of projective path-geometry type. It descends to an ordinary projective equivalence
class of torsion-free affine connections on the two-dimensional point space precisely
when F is cubic or lower in p = y′. Thus a projective connection on contact elements
and one on points are different notions.
Reconstruction. We preserve the passage to contact elements rather than auto-
matically declaring the general-F sentence false. The surface-connection reading
requires the cubic condition recorded later on P000666.
Fate. Cartan (1924, introduction and §§VII–VIII, especially nos.22–24) explicitly dis-
tinguishes the two geometries and proves the general contact-element construc-
tion.

1976-403.4
Two transverse line fields spanning a contact
distribution
Class E
Kind; source. construction; P000665
The notes. Arnold says that reducing a pair of direction fields in R3 under diffeomor-
phisms is equivalent to the ODE problem, then remarks that their span is a contact
structure.
Statement. Let L, V be an ordered pair of smooth rank-one distributions on a three-
manifold, transverse at the point, with L ⊕V contact. Locally choose contact coor-
dinates (x, y, p) in which V = ⟨∂p⟩and L = ⟨∂x + p∂y + F∂p⟩. Local diffeomorphisms
preserving the ordered pair correspond to point transformations on the local leaf
space of V , with their prolongations. Arbitrary pairs whose span is integrable are
excluded.
Reconstruction. The rank, ordering, contact and local leaf-space assumptions are
restored explicitly. Exchanging the two fields gives the dual equation rather than
the same point-equivalence problem.
Fate. The contact-element formulation and duality are in Cartan (1924, §VII and
§VIII, no.23).
Present status. As of 10 November 2026: The regular contact version is exact. With-
out the contact hypothesis the announced equivalence is false.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:

1976-403.6
Inequivalent projective structures on the circle
Class E
Kind; source. construction; P000665
The notes. The standard RP1 and its pullback under a degree-two covering S1 →RP1
are called inequivalent. Arnold proposes examining infinitesimal automorphisms
∂x, x∂x, x2∂x.
Statement. For each positive integer n, pull back the projective atlas on RP1 by the
degree-n covering. The resulting circle structures are pairwise inequivalent under
projective-structure-preserving diffeomorphisms. In an angular coordinate their in-
finitesimal projective automorphism fields are spanned by ∂θ, sin(nθ)∂θ, cos(nθ)∂θ. A
nonzero such field can have at most 2n distinct zeros, and the maximum is achieved.
Reconstruction. [proved here] The zero-count maximum is preserved by diffeomor-
phisms, distinguishing n = 1 from n = 2 as the notebook requests. The abstract Lie
algebra is sl2 in both cases; its realization on the circle, not its abstract isomorphism
type, distinguishes them.
Fate. This is an elementary consequence of the classical projective action; Cartan
(1924, projective frame construction) supplies the historical framework. No com-
plete classification of all circle projective structures is claimed here.
Present status. As of 10 November 2026: The two stated examples, and their cov-
ering generalization, are rigorously distinguished by the displayed argument.
Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: partial; verification:
read the result; development: annotated; confidence: high

1976-403.10
Why vanishing on one solution does not imply
formal straightening
Class E
Kind; source. claim; P000666
The notes. The recorded theorem says that if a(x)(dx)5/2 ≡0, the equation can be
straightened in formal series.
The page has just fixed a single solution; no “all
solutions” or cubic hypothesis is stated in the theorem line.
Statement. Taken along one chosen solution, the implication is false: y′′ = y3 has the
solution y = 0, zero linear and quadratic transverse parts, and hence zero displayed
density there, but its Tresse invariant is H = 36y, a nonzero formal series. It is
therefore not formally point-equivalent to Y ′′ = 0 near that solution. A sufficient
corrected condition is the simultaneous formal vanishing of I, H; in the cubic-in-p
class, vanishing of H on an open set suffices because I = 0.
Reconstruction. [proved here] This counterexample audits the strongest literal in-
terpretation supported by the neighbouring sentences. We do not claim it disproves
an unstated theorem about a family of all solutions with additional assumptions.
Fate. The obstruction is already part of classical point equivalence (Kruglikov, 2009,

1976-403.11
The normal form with a squared first derivative
factor
Class E
Kind; source. method; P000666
The notes. Further diffeomorphisms are said to lead to y′′ = y′2A(x, y, y′).
Statement. Every regular smooth scalar second-order equation admits this form
locally near a chosen initial direction after a point transformation. First choose a
local foliation by solutions and use its leaf label as y, obtaining F(x, y, 0) = 0. Then
put X = φ(x, y), Y = y, with φx ̸= 0 and φxx = Fp(x, y, 0)φx. The transformed right

by smooth division.
Reconstruction. [proved here] The foliation exists by the local ODE existence the-
orem and a transverse initial curve. The second coordinate change is obtained by
integrating a linear equation for φx, choosing it nonzero. This proof supplies the
precise local scope omitted in the notes.

1976-403.12
Cubic slope equations form an invariant projective
class
Class E
Kind; source. theorem; P000666
The notes. The equation y′′ = P3(y′), with coefficients depending on x, y, is said to be

Statement. The class F = A0(x, y)+A1(x, y)p+A2(x, y)p2+A3(x, y)p3 is preserved by local
point transformations wherever the transformed independent variable is valid. Ex-
actly these equations describe the unparametrized geodesics of torsion-free affine
connections on a surface, modulo projective equivalence b∇XY = ∇XY + α(X)Y +
α(Y )X.
Reconstruction. The coefficients may vary on the surface; “third degree” means de-
gree at most 3. The graph-coordinate restriction excludes directions sent to vertical
ones.
Fate. Cartan (1924, introduction, §VIII no.24) explicitly gives the cubic criterion;
Kruglikov (2009, §4.1) supplies a modern account of Lie and Liouville’s earlier work.
Present status. As of 10 November 2026: Established projective differential geom-
etry, already classical in 1976.
Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: exact; verification: read
the result; development: none; confidence: high

1976-403.13

Class E

The notes. The scalar-invariant discussion begins with v(x) = a(x)−2/5∂x.
Statement. On an oriented interval where the real density coefficient A is nowhere

1.8
S. P. Novikov, 1976-09-20: Lie superalgebras and the
Wess–Zumino multiplet
record 404; source pages P000667-P000669.
Novikov explicitly recommends Ogievetsky’s UFN review and a Zumino review,
with Volkov and Akulov noted in the margin. He defines Lie superalgebras, uses real
Majorana spinors to construct supertranslations, and records Gelfand’s request to
adjoin Lorentz generators. The Wess–Zumino chiral multiplet, its auxiliary fields, La-
grangian and infinitesimal transformations occupy the remainder. The material pre-
dates the talk, as the cited 1975 review confirms. Several faithful notebook errors
require emendation: Gol’fand’s name, the scalar label on the spinor, an uncontracted
kinetic index, and incompatible coefficients between the two action formulas.

1976-404.2
The associative supercommutator
Class E
Kind; source. construction; P000667-P000668
The notes. Even-even and even-odd brackets are commutators; odd-odd brackets
are anticommutators in an associative matrix algebra.
Statement. For an associative Z/2-graded algebra A with AiAj ⊂Ai+j, [a, b]s = ab −
(−1)|a||b|ba defines a Lie superalgebra. Any graded linear subspace closed under this
bracket is a Lie subsuperalgebra.
A matrix example requires a chosen grading,
such as the block grading of End(V0 ⊕V1). Closure under associative multiplication
is not required for the subsuperalgebra.
Reconstruction. The grading of the ambient associative algebra is made explicit;
arbitrary ungraded matrix subspaces with an assigned parity need not work.
Fate. This standard realization is used in Ogievetsky and Mezincescu (1975, §2);
see also the earlier occurrence 1975-389.5.
Present status. As of 10 November 2026: Established directly by expanding the
graded Jacobi identity.
Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: exact; verification: read
the result; development: none; confidence: high
1976-404.3
Real Majorana spinors in signature (-,+,+,+)
Class E
Kind; source. construction; P000668
The notes. The metric is diag(−1, 1, 1, 1), the spinor space is explicitly corrected to
real dimension 4, and four real matrices satisfy γµγν + γνγµ = 2ηµν. A barred spinor
is written by multiplication by γ0.
Statement. For this signature, the real Clifford algebra Cl(3, 1) is isomorphic to M4(R)

¯s = sTC. The covariance formulas depend on this convention.
Reconstruction. The transpose and charge-conjugation matrix distinguish the al-

nions” is only an analogy, not the Clifford-algebra identification.
Fate. The Majorana conventions used for the component theory are discussed in
Ogievetsky and Mezincescu (1975, §3 and Appendix).
Present status. As of 10 November 2026: The real four-component construction is

Class E

Statement. Let V = R1,3 and S a real Majorana spin module. A nonzero Spin(1, 3)-

has {Qα, ¯Q ˙β} = 2σµ
α ˙βPµ, {Q, Q} = { ¯Q, ¯Q} = 0.

1976-404.5
Extending supertranslations to the Poincaré

Class E
Kind; source. problem; P000668; I. M. Gelfand
The notes. Gelfand asks to extend the Poincaré algebra to a superalgebra.
The
preceding historical sentence names “Gelfand and Likhtman”.
Statement. Adjoin so(1, 3) to the supertranslation algebra by its vector action on V

Thus s0 = iso(1, 3), s1 = S, giving the
four-dimensional N = 1 super-Poincaré algebra without central charges.

actually written, not a classification of all possible extensions. The historical name
is Yu. A. Gol’fand, not the seminar’s I. M. Gelfand.
Fate. Likhtman (2001, Appendix, equation 1 and release note) reproduces a preprint
passed for printing 12 April 1971; its references identify the Gol’fand–Likhtman
1971 publication.
The requested extension was therefore already known before
this talk.
Present status. As of 10 November 2026: Solved by the displayed semidirect con-

Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: exact; verification: read

1.9
A. A. Migdal, 1976-09-20: Why quarks suggest local
colour gauge fields
record 405; source pages P000669.
Migdal’s short discussion begins after Novikov’s variations on P000669. It moves
from three-quark nucleons and quark–antiquark mesons to short-distance freedom,
confinement, relativistic locality and the colour/flavour matrix. No QCD field equa-
tion is actually written. Its force-law and uncertainty-principle slogans are not math-
ematical confinement proofs. The matrix uses three light flavours and three colours;
it should not be interpreted as nine independent flavours or as an exhaustive 1976
particle inventory. Bernstein’s dated 18/X-76 lecture starts only on P000670 and is
outside this record.

1976-405.3
Confinement as a pre-existing physical programme
Class E
Kind; source. claim; P000669
The notes. The juxtaposed assertion is that quarks cannot be taken out of a nucleon

Statement. The intended physical hypothesis is absence of isolated colour-charged

by this page. Cutoff-lattice strong-coupling confinement and continuum QCD con-

1976-405.4

Class E
Kind; source. claim; P000669
The notes. The emphatic sentence is:
В теории силы должны расти с расстояниями!
Statement. In a static-source model, a confining potential V (R) ∼σR, σ > 0, has force

1976-405.7
Colour and flavour are two different indices
Class E
Kind; source. construction; P000669
The notes. The 3 × 3 array has columns p, n, λ and rows 1,2,3. The vertical group
is SU(3) colour, the horizontal flavour group is annotated “unknown”, and the text
says there are nine quarks.
Statement. For the three light flavours, write qa
f, a = 1, 2, 3, f = u, d, s. This gives nine
colour–flavour components before spinor indices; it does not give nine flavours. A
local colour matrix acts on a, while a global flavour matrix acts on f. With equal
light-quark masses and electromagnetism omitted, vector SU(3) flavour is a sym-
metry; unequal masses break it. The full modern quark inventory has six flavours,
each with three colours.
Reconstruction. The traditional p, n, λ labels are interpreted as the light-flavour
triplet, not physical proton/neutron/Lambda fields in the same gauge multiplet. The
“unknown” annotation does not specify a unique unresolved group-classification
problem.
Fate. Gross and Wilczek (1973, p.1345) already separates colour from global flavour
symmetries.
Amsler et al. (2024, §§15.1–15.2,15.5.1) gives modern terminology.
The seminar had already discussed four flavours and three colours in 1975-367.5.
Present status. As of 10 November 2026: The nine-component light-flavour model is
identifiable. Treating it as a complete inventory in 1976 would contradict the earlier
seminar’s four-flavour discussion; the shorthand is therefore not silently expanded
into such a claim.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
partial; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high

1976-405.8
Local colour gauge symmetry
Class E
Kind; source. definition; P000669
The notes. The last conceptual distinction says that the colour group corresponds
to a much stronger symmetry, namely gauge symmetry.
Statement. The gauge group consists locally of smooth maps U : M →SU(3), acting
by qf 7→Uqf and, for D = d + A, A 7→UAU −1 −(dU)U −1. Then F 7→UFU −1. Constant
colour rotations form a subgroup of this local transformation group. “Stronger”
means freedom to choose the colour frame independently at each spacetime point,
with the connection compensating derivatives.
Reconstruction. This specifies the mathematical content of the comparison; gauge
transformations describe redundancy of field variables and are not simply an extra
independent global flavour action.
Fate. Yang and Mills (1954, isotopic gauge transformation, equations 1–5) gives the
local construction in 1954; the colour specialization predates the talk.
Present status. As of 10 November 2026: Established local gauge symmetry. No
new gauge-group extension problem is posed by the sentence.
Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: exact; verification: read
the result; development: none; confidence: high
1.10
I. N. Bernstein, 1976-10-18: Superdomains, Berezin
integration, and Stokes boundaries
record 406; source pages P000670-P000674.
The dated continuation begins on P000670, after a separate question about factors.
Bernstein explains superfunctions, parity-preserving morphisms, nilpotent Taylor ex-
pansion, and why a boundary requires an embedded subsupermanifold in addition to
its ordinary body. The circle and half-line examples lead to the Stokes problem, soon
printed by Bernstein and Leites. P000673–P000674 explicitly form a retrospective
summary of earlier super talks copied from I. Dorfman’s notebook; their definitions
and linear algebra are indexed here with that provenance, rather than assigned a new
seminar date. The catalogue’s P000668–P000675 span also includes adjoining talks.

1976-406.4
Morphisms determined by coordinate pullbacks
Class E
Kind; source. theorem; P000670; P000674
The notes. A map covers a map of the bodies and induces a unital algebra homo-
morphism in the reverse direction preserving parity. The retrospective page says
that it suffices to specify v∗
i , η∗
j , and gives v = u + ξ1ξ2, η1 = uξ1, η2 = ξ2.
Statement. A morphism Φ : U p|q →V m|n is a body map together with a parity-
preserving local unital homomorphism of sheaves Φ♯: OV →Φ∗OU. It is uniquely
specified by even sections a1, . . . , am and odd sections β1, . . . , βn, provided the body
tuple (a1,0, . . . , am,0) takes values in V . Smooth functions of the ai are evaluated by
their finite nilpotent Taylor expansion.

essential additions. The example with η1 = uξ1 is a morphism but is not an invertible

and Leites (1977, §3).
Present status. As of November 2026, the coordinate theorem is standard; invert-

1976-406.5
Endomorphisms of a point with two odd directions
Class E
Kind; source. construction; P000670
The notes. For p = 0, q = 2 the underlying space is a point. The allowed images of
ξ1, ξ2 are arbitrary linear combinations of the odd generators; a term proportional
to ξ1ξ2 is crossed out because parity must be preserved.
Statement. Every parity-preserving unital R-algebra endomorphism of Λ(ξ1, ξ2) is
determined by ξ1 7→aξ1 + bξ2, ξ2 7→cξ1 + dξ2. Any real a, b, c, d are allowed; the endo-
morphism is an automorphism exactly when ad −bc ̸= 0. A quadratic term cannot
occur in the image of an odd generator.
Reconstruction. The automorphism criterion is an elementary consequence, not a
claim explicitly written on the page. It clarifies the distinction between an endo-
morphism and a coordinate change.
Fate. This is the 0|2 instance of the pre-existing coordinate-morphism formalism in
Berezin and Leites (1975, Definition 3); compare Bernstein and Leites (1977, §3).
Present status. As of November 2026, this finite exterior-algebra example is com-
pletely determined by its odd degree-one part.
Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: exact; verification: read

1976-406.10
Berezinian volume elements rather than a top
ordinary form
Class E
Kind; source. definition; P000671; P000674
The notes. The notes say that one integrates densities transforming by the
Berezinian, and define ρ = fD(x), D(y)/D(x) = Ber J.
They contrast these with
ordinary differential forms.
Statement. For an oriented smooth p|q supermanifold, the Berezinian line has local
generator D(u, ξ) and transition law D(y) = Ber(∂y/∂x)D(x), with coefficients pulled
back to a common chart. Thus a section satisfies fx = (fy ◦Φ) Ber DΦ. For q > 0,
the usual super differential-form algebra has no maximal form degree, since the
differentials of odd coordinates commute in exterior degree. A Berezinian section
is therefore not a highest ordinary differential form.
Reconstruction. Orientation and coefficient pullback are explicit here. The state-
ment “forms cannot be integrated” is read as the lack of this canonical top-form
construction, not a prohibition of every integration operation on superforms.
Fate. The density law is already Berezin and Leites (1975, Definition 5 and for-
mula (3)); Bernstein and Leites (1977, §§5–7) distinguishes volume, differential,
and integral forms.
Present status. As of November 2026, the distinction is standard and is essential to
the Stokes formulation below.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
exact; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high

1976-406.12
Regular ideals and embedded even boundaries
Class E
Kind; source. definition; P000671-P000672
The notes. A subsupermanifold is described by an ideal of functions; a boundary
is cut out by an even superfunction f = 0. The notes emphasize that it carries the
structure of a subsupermanifold.
Statement. An embedded codimension r|s subsupermanifold is locally given by r
even and s odd functions whose body Jacobian has the corresponding full even and
odd ranks, so that they extend to a super-coordinate system. In particular an even f
with df0 ̸= 0 on f−1
0 (0) defines a codimension 1|0 boundary. An arbitrary ideal defines
a ringed subspace, not necessarily a subsupermanifold.

the ideal description.
Fate. The regular-equation result predates the talk: Berezin and Leites (1975, The-
orem 2). The boundary-specific formulation is Bernstein and Leites (1977, §§8, 11).
Present status. As of November 2026, regularity separates smooth embedded
boundaries from singular quotient spaces.

1.11
V. N. Popov [catalogue; attribution conflict],
1976-10-25: Connections, free Yang–Mills fields,
and Ricci-flat metrics
record 407; source pages P000675-P000677.
The notes begin on P000675 with Gelfand’s request for an elementary explanation
of a connection and a reference to Yang and Mills. They develop gauge covariance, the
principal-bundle equation HδhΦ = 0, its independence of the auxiliary vertical metric,
and the affine-frame realization of the vacuum Einstein equations. The last two results
match D. A. Popov’s 1975 paper and D. A. Popov–L. I. Daikhin’s 1975 note exactly.
The supplied catalogue calls the speaker V. N. Popov, while the handwritten heading
gives only Popov; that conflict is retained explicitly. Mathematical identification of
the papers does not prove a correction of the speaker’s initials. The identified notes
occupy P000675–P000677, narrower than the catalogue’s P000673–P000679 packet.
1976-407.1
What is a connection?
Class E
Kind; source. question; P000675-P000676; I. M. Gelfand
The notes. Gelfand asks Что такое связность? (для первокурсников и Полякова)
and lists Nomizu, Sternberg, Bishop–Crittenden, and Landau–Lifshitz. Later the
talk represents a connection by a Lie-algebra-valued one-form Ωon a principal bun-
dle.
Statement. For a smooth right principal G-bundle π : P →M, a connection is a
smooth right-equivariant horizontal subbundle H ⊂TP with TP = H ⊕ker dπ. Equiv-
alently it is Ω∈Ω1(P; g) such that Ω(a#) = a for each fundamental vertical field and
R∗
gΩ= Ad(g−1)Ω. Local potentials are s∗Ωfor local sections s.
Reconstruction. The two connection axioms and their equivalent horizontal-space
interpretation are supplied. Gelfand’s request is pedagogical, not a new research
question; no specific edition of the reading-list books is inferred.
Fate. The exact principal-connection interpretation is recalled in Popov and Daikhin
(1975, §1, Definition 1) and in Popov (1975, §1), both published before the seminar.
Present status. As of November 2026, the question is answered by the standard
principal-connection definition.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
exact; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high
1976-407.2
The massless Dirac equation used to introduce gauge
coupling
Class E
Kind; source. definition; P000675
The notes. The opening equation is the massless Dirac equation for an ordinary
four-component spinor. No interaction term is included in that displayed equation.
Statement. On four-dimensional Minkowski space with a fixed spin structure,
choose matrices γµ satisfying γµγν + γνγµ = 2gµνI. A free massless spinor satisfies
iγµ∂µΨ = 0, equivalently the equation without the factor i. A gauge coupling in a
representation R replaces ∂µ by Dµ = ∂µ + R∗(Aµ).
Reconstruction. The Clifford relation and a definite signature convention are
needed to interpret the written symbols. The covariant derivative is explanatory
reconstruction; the notebook does not yet state a coupled Dirac–Yang–Mills system.
Fate. The free Dirac equation was classical long before 1976. The local-symmetry
motivation for replacing the derivative is the construction in Yang and Mills (1954,
“Isotopic gauge transformation,” pp. 192–193); the publication match is thematic
for the notebook’s isolated massless equation.

Present status. As of November 2026, this is established background, and the dis-
played free spinor equation does not by itself couple matter to the later gauge field.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
thematic only; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high

1976-407.5
The distinction between a gauge field and quark
matter
Class E
Kind; source. claim; P000675
The notes. The notebook says Ур-ия Янга–Миллса описывают кварки immediately
after the source-free gauge equations and the earlier free Dirac equation.
Statement. The displayed unknown A is a Lie-algebra-valued gauge potential.
Quark fields are spinor-valued matter fields carrying a color representation; a
classical model with such matter requires a covariant Dirac equation and a current
term DµF µν = jν. Consequently the source-free equation written here describes
the gauge sector, not the full coupled quark dynamics.
Reconstruction. The phrase may be broad physical motivation, but as a literal identi-
fication of the displayed equations with quark fields it is false. Neither confinement
nor a statement about individual observed quarks follows.
Fate. The original Yang and Mills (1954, pp. 192–194) distinguishes the gauge po-
tential from the matter wavefunction and its current; the note’s free-field terminol-
ogy is also explicit in Popov and Daikhin (1975, Definitions 1–3). The correction
concerns that pre-existing distinction.
Present status. As of November 2026, the literal field identification requires correc-
tion; no unresolved research assertion is extracted from the motivational sentence.
Assessment. literal statement: false as written; publication match: partial; verifi-
cation: read the result; development: annotated; confidence: high
1976-407.6
The abelian reduction and gradient gauge
transformations
Class E
Kind; source. theorem; P000675
The notes. For G = U(1), the notes obtain Maxwell’s equations and write Aµ 7→
Aµ + ∂µα. The antisymmetric matrix separates electric and magnetic components.
Statement. For an abelian connection, F = dA locally, so dF = 0 identically and
the source-free Yang–Mills equation is d∗F = 0.
These are the vacuum Maxwell
equations. A local gauge change A 7→A + dα leaves F unchanged; globally A need
not be a single one-form when the U(1)-bundle is nontrivial.
Reconstruction. The Bianchi equation supplies the homogeneous half of Maxwell’s
system. Metric raising of both indices is required: the formula with derivatives of
Aν directly defines Fµν, not F µν in Minkowski coordinates. Electric/magnetic signs
depend on the metric and time-coordinate convention.
Fate. The comparison with electromagnetism is explicit in Yang and Mills (1954,
pp. 192–193) and Popov and Daikhin (1975, §1, after Definition 3), both before this
talk.
Present status. As of November 2026, the abelian reduction is exact, subject to the
local-potential and index conventions stated here.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
exact; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high

1976-407.8
A metric on the total space of a principal bundle
Class E
Kind; source. construction; P000676
The notes. Given g on M, a connection Ω, and an arbitrary nondegenerate quadratic
form K on g, the notes set h = π∗g +K(Ω, Ω), observing that it is generally not invari-
ant.
Statement. Let g be nondegenerate and symmetric on TM, and K a fixed nondegen-
erate symmetric form on g. The formula
hp(X, Y ) = gπ(p)(dπX, dπY ) + K(ΩX, ΩY )
defines a smooth nondegenerate pseudo-Riemannian metric on P: horizontal and
vertical directions are orthogonal, with metrics g and K.
It is right G-invariant
exactly when K is Ad(G)-invariant.
Reconstruction. Symmetry is made explicit because the source calls K a quadratic
form.
Semisimplicity is not needed for this auxiliary metric construction; it is
needed only if one insists on taking the Killing form.
Fate. The semisimple metric is in Popov (1975, equation (22)); the arbitrary-group
extension using an arbitrary nondegenerate form appears explicitly in Popov and
Daikhin (1975, §1, before Lemma 1), printed in 1975.
Present status. As of November 2026, the construction is valid without an invariant
vertical metric, as the notes correctly emphasize.
Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: exact; verification: read
the result; development: none; confidence: high
1976-407.9
Horizontal curvature and the structural identity
Class E
Kind; source. definition; P000676
The notes. Curvature is defined by Φ = HdΩ= dΩ+ 1
2[Ω, Ω], where H takes the hori-
zontal component. A horizontal form vanishes when one argument is vertical.
Statement. For a principal connection, Φ = dΩ+ 1
2[Ω∧Ω] is horizontal and Ad-
equivariant. Hence it is equivalently a two-form on M valued in the adjoint bundle.
Its covariant exterior derivative vanishes: DΦ = 0, the Bianchi identity.
Reconstruction. The bracket includes the wedge product and the Lie bracket. The
Bianchi identity is stated here to distinguish curvature geometry from the additional
field equation; it is implicit in the later comparison with Maxwell’s equations.
Fate. These standard connection identities are used in Popov (1975, §1) and restated
in Popov and Daikhin (1975, §1), both preceding the seminar.
Present status. As of November 2026, the curvature and Bianchi identities are es-
tablished independently of any field equation.
Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: exact; verification: read
the result; development: none; confidence: high

1976-407.10
The projected codifferential is independent of the
vertical metric
Class E
Kind; source. theorem; P000676-P000677
The notes. The notes propose HδhΦ = 0 and emphasize that it does not depend on
K. A parenthetical sentence makes the broader assertion for equivariant horizontal
vector-valued forms.
Statement. For the metric h above and a principal-connection curvature Φ, HδhΦ is
horizontal, Ad-equivariant, and independent of the chosen nondegenerate K. Thus
it defines an intrinsic equation DΦ = 0 for any Lie group. For semisimple G, this
coincides with the Euler–Lagrange Yang–Mills equation formed using the Killing
metric.
Reconstruction. The exact verified theorem is for the curvature form. The wider
notebook parenthesis about arbitrary equivariant horizontal forms is not needed for
the field equation and is not asserted here without specifying its representation and
codifferential conventions. No invariant K is imposed on the curvature theorem:
that would improperly weaken the result.
Fate. The semisimple equivalence is Popov (1975, Proposition 3). The arbitrary-
group, arbitrary-K assertion is exactly Popov and Daikhin (1975, Lemma 1 and Def-
inition 3, p. 791), received 26 June 1975 and published in 1975. It is therefore E,
not a post-seminar development.
Present status. As of November 2026, the verified curvature equation is established
for arbitrary Lie groups; the scope of the broader parenthetical claim is kept sepa-
rate.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
partial; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high

1976-407.13
Vacuum Einstein equations as the affine curvature
equation
Class E
Kind; source. theorem; P000677
The notes. On the metric-induced Cartan connections, HδΦ = 0 is identified with
Ric = 0. The notes underline that these are first order in the connection.
Statement. For the affine connection ¯Ωinduced by a pseudo-Riemannian metric as
above,
Hδh ¯Φ = 0
⇐⇒
Ric(g) = 0.
In the splitting affn = gln ⋉Rn, the translational component of the projected equation
is, up to the fixed Hodge convention, contraction of the Ricci tensor; the linear
component is the covariant-divergence equation for curvature.
Reconstruction. The equivalence holds on the specified metric-induced, torsion-free
affine connections.
It is false if one replaces them merely by linear-frame Levi-
Civita connections: their Yang–Mills equation alone gives harmonic curvature, not
necessarily Ricci flatness. The assertion is about vacuum equations with zero cos-
mological constant.
Fate. This is exactly Popov and Daikhin (1975, Theorem 2 and equation (5), p. 792),
printed in 1975. The source explicitly contrasts its affine result with Yang’s linear-
frame equation.
Present status. As of November 2026, the equivalence is settled with those con-
straints; it is not an equivalence between all unrestricted Yang–Mills solutions and
all Einstein metrics.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
exact; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high
1976-407.14
Why the second-order connection equation follows
from Ricci flatness
Class E
Kind; source. claim; P000677
The notes. A final diagram links Rij = 0 with the equation Rij,k = Rik,j; the second-
order equations are called consequences of the first-order equations.
Statement. For a Levi-Civita connection, the contracted differential Bianchi identity
expresses the divergence of Riemann curvature as the antisymmetrized covariant
derivative of Ricci, with index signs depending on the curvature convention. Hence
Ric = 0 implies ∇j Ricik = ∇k Ricij and D∗R = 0. Ricci uses one derivative of the con-
nection coefficients, whereas its covariant derivative uses two. In metric variables
their orders are two and three respectively.
Reconstruction. The derivative-order assertion is relative to connection coefficients
and to the metric-induced constraint.
The implication is one-way for the linear-


===== PDF PAGE 43 =====
CHAPTER 1. THE ACADEMIC YEARS 1976–77 AND 1977–78
43
frame equation; a parallel nonzero Ricci tensor also satisfies the displayed deriva-
tive identity.
Fate. The consequence and the fact that the translational component is fixed are
stated immediately after Popov and Daikhin (1975, Theorem 2). This is the same
pre-1976 observation, not an additional field equation discovered at the talk.
Present status. As of November 2026, the implication follows from the differential
Bianchi identity; its converse requires the affine translational equation.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
exact; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high
1.12
I. N. Bernstein, 1976-11-22: Euler products,
compact-group heat kernels, and affine Lie algebras
record 408; source pages P000678-P000680.
Bernstein explains Euler and Jacobi identities, Macdonald’s root-system general-
ization, and Kostant’s expression through characters and the Casimir. A shifted spec-
tral zeta function is introduced, but its displayed reflection equation lacks the nec-
essary Mellin completion. Olshanetsky intervenes on radial Laplacians and quantum
integrability. Bernstein then gives the Cartan-matrix construction and the affine A2
loop-algebra example. The date 22 November is supplied by the catalogue; the cor-
responding handwritten start on P000678 is undated and P000680 ends with a New
Year greeting. This evidence does not establish additional sessions within the packet.
The mathematical notes are P000678–P000680, rather than the catalogue’s wider
P000677–P000681 span.

The invariant Casimir and the compact-group
Laplacian
Class E
Kind; source. construction; P000678
The notes. For compact semisimple G, a Killing-orthonormal basis Xi is used to write
∆= P X2
i , asserted to be two-sided invariant and scalar on irreducible representa-
tions.
Statement. Let G be a connected compact semisimple Lie group and choose
X1, . . . , Xd orthonormal for minus the Killing form. The operator ∆= P
i X2
i , acting
as left-invariant vector fields, is a nonpositive bi-invariant Laplacian. Its negative
C = −∆is the positive Casimir and acts on a finite-dimensional irreducible repre-

1976-408.7
The distinguished regular conjugacy class in SU(n)
Class E
Kind; source. construction; P000679
The notes. For SU(n), the notes describe a unique regular conjugacy class whose
nth power is scalar, represented by c diag(ξ, ξ2, . . . , ξn), with c chosen for determinant
one.
Statement. Take a primitive nth root ζ and choose c ∈U(1) so that cnζn(n+1)/2 = 1.
Then a = c diag(ζ, . . . , ζn) ∈SU(n), has distinct eigenvalues, and an is scalar. Con-
versely, if a ∈SU(n) has distinct eigenvalues and scalar nth power, its eigenvalues
form the full set of roots of one scalar; the determinant condition makes it conjugate
to this element. Different permissible choices of c merely permute that set.
Reconstruction. Primitivity of ζ and the distinction between one element and one
conjugacy class are explicit. The short converse is an elementary reconstruction
from the written characterization.
Fate. This is the type A principal-element case of the already circulating Kostant
description, recorded in Demazure (1977, §3); the canonical type-ρ element is also
defined before Kostant (2003, Theorem 0.1).

1976-408.12
A complete commuting family of quantum integrals
from radial operators
Class A
Kind; source. claim; P000679; M. A. Olshanetsky
The notes. Olshanetsky adds that there is a complete system of observables “in the
quantum sense.” No domains or specific higher operators are written.
Statement. For the Hamiltonians obtained from symmetric-space radial parts with
the group values of the coupling constants, the radial invariant differential opera-
tors form a commutative algebra. Conjugating this algebra by the same J1/2 gives
commuting differential operators including the Hamiltonian and having r = dim a
algebraically independent principal symbols. This is formal quantum complete in-
tegrability on the regular chamber.
Reconstruction. The precise assertion concerns commuting differential expressions

is not inferred.
Fate. This is the group-coupling assertion of Olshanetsky and Perelomov (1978,
§4, Theorem 2 and the preceding commutativity corollary), received in 1977 and
printed in 1978. The paper gives explicit generators for several root systems in §5.
Present status. As of November 2026, the formal commuting algebra is established

1976-408.13
Generalized Cartan matrices
Class E
Kind; source. definition; P000679
The notes. The notebook requires integer entries, aii = 2, symmetric occurrence of
zeros, but writes aij < 0 for every i ̸= j.
Statement. A generalized Cartan matrix is an integer matrix A = (aij) with aii = 2,
aij ≤0 for i ̸= j, and aij = 0 if and only if aji = 0. It is symmetrizable if DA is symmetric
for a positive diagonal matrix D.
Reconstruction. The strict inequality is a faithful error: it contradicts the adjacent
comment about zeros and would exclude disconnected diagrams and most ordinary
Dynkin diagrams. Symmetrizability is additional structure, not part of the three
basic conditions.
Fate. The conditions are classical before the talk: see Kac (1968, Chapter II, §1,
conditions (M2)–(M5)) and Demazure (1977, §5).
Present status. As of November 2026, the corrected nonpositive-off-diagonal defi-
nition is standard.

1.14
Yu. I. Manin, 1977-02-14: Spectral curves, rank-two
solitons, and filtered bimodules
record 410; source pages P000681-P000684.
Manin begins with a reference to Calogero’s boomeron, then describes a fourth-
order Lax system and an explicitly singular, periodically oscillating rank-two pulse.
The algebraic part passes from commuting operators to a spectral curve and eigen-
function bundles, contrasts rank-one Jacobian motion with higher-rank bundle moduli,
and gives the filtered-bimodule construction and its inverse. Two further connections
yield evolution and zero-curvature equations. The material has an unusually close
later match in Manin’s 1978 survey, including a sign error in the flatness formula that
survives into the printed English translation. The last sentence announces Duister-
maat for the next meeting; it does not contain that lecture.

1976-410.5
Commuting operators, spectral curves, and
eigenfunction sheaves
Class E
Kind; source. method; P000681-P000682
The notes. Manin first constructs [L1, P1] = 0, then solves L1ψ = λψ, P1ψ = µψ over
an affine algebraic curve X. The sketch puts the common-eigenfunction space over
(λ, µ), and the next page extends the bundle to a completion.
Statement. For commuting scalar ordinary differential operators of positive orders
over a differential field of characteristic zero with algebraically closed constant
field, the Burchnall–Chaundy relation gives a nonzero polynomial Q over the con-
stants with Q(L1, P1) = 0. On a component of the resulting spectral curve where
the joint-eigenspace dimension is constant, these eigenspaces form the spectral
bundle. Compactification and normalization, with a specified point at infinity and
suitable local data, give the algebro-geometric spectral data. At singularities one
must allow a torsion-free sheaf rather than automatically a vector bundle.
Reconstruction. The scalar, constant-field, generic-rank and completion qualifica-
tions are supplied. The sketch is a fibre diagram, not evidence that every singular
joint spectrum has an everywhere locally free eigenbundle. The matrix-coefficient
case later in the talk is handled through the bimodule axioms, not by an unrestricted
extension of the scalar theorem. Earlier-index continuity: 1975-391.1. These links
identify a recurring statement or construction; only the explicitly indexed inverse
problem reuses a problem ID.
Fate. Krichever (1976, §2, proposition on p. 293) already states the polynomial rela-
tion and rank-one inverse construction; it was received 8 October and presented 24
October 1975, then published in 1976. Krichever (1978, Theorem 1.1, pp. 176–177)
explicitly attributes the relation to Burchnall and Chaundy. This continues earlier
item 1975-391.1.
Present status. As of November 2026, the qualified spectral-curve method is estab-
lished; the singular-fibre caveat remains necessary.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
exact; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high

arbitrary higher-rank bundles. Earlier-index continuity: 1975-384.3, 1975-385.8.
These links identify a recurring statement or construction; only the explicitly in-
dexed inverse problem reuses a problem ID.
Fate. The rank-one construction is already Krichever (1976, §1, Theorem 1 and
