1976-410.7
The recurring higher-rank inverse construction
Class B
Kind; source. question; P000681-P000682
The notes. The notes contrast dim{ψ} = 1 with r > 1, refer to Drinfeld’s nonexplicit
description and difficulties, and replace the Jacobian by a moduli space of bundles.
They do not give a higher-rank reconstruction formula here.
Statement. The precise recurring inverse problem is to recover commuting scalar
ordinary differential operators of generic spectral rank r > 1 from appropriate
pointed-curve and rank-r spectral-bundle data. In the nonsingular-curve, general-
position setting, one must include a local parameter, a normalized matrix divisor
(Tyurin data), and the additional functional parameters; the curve and an unframed
bundle alone do not determine the operators.
Reconstruction. The inverse-problem formulation is reconstructed from the con-
trast with the preceding construction and from earlier occurrences 1975-391.3–4.
It is narrower than a claim that every holomorphic path in every bundle-moduli
space yields a Lax solution. The data and genericity here follow the later effec-
tive reconstruction, not an unrecorded theorem attributed to Manin. Earlier-index
continuity: 1975-391.3, 1975-391.4. These links identify a recurring statement or
construction; only the explicitly indexed inverse problem reuses a problem ID.
Fate. Drinfeld (1977, Proposition 2), received 14 April 1976 and printed in the first
1977 issue, gives the abstract geometric correspondence; the precise public issue
date relative to 14 February is not established. Krichever (1978, §2, Lemma 2.2 and
Theorem 2.3, pp. 181–182) gives an effective reconstruction for smooth spectral
curves in general position. Its introduction expressly corrects serious errors in the
1977 implementation; it was submitted 15 February 1977, the day after this talk,
and printed in 1978. The class B assignment refers to that effective reconstruction
by another author, not to discovery of the older abstract correspondence.
Present status. As of November 2026, the stated generic inverse problem is solved.
Singular spectral curves, special strata, and global real regularity are separate
questions, not residual open claims assigned to this notebook.

1976-410.8
Hyperelliptic curves and the two-quadrics shorthand
Class F
Kind; source. claim; P000682
The notes. Immediately after the higher-rank moduli remark, the page says
Гиперэлл. кривая −→пересеч. 2-х квадрик в C5.
It does not specify a genus,
rank, determinant, or which moduli space the arrow denotes.
Statement. A precise plausible referent is the classical theorem that for a smooth
projective complex curve C of genus two and a fixed line bundle Λ of odd degree, the
moduli space of stable rank-two bundles with determinant Λ is a smooth intersection
of two quadrics in P5. The notebook does not supply enough data to identify that
theorem uniquely, and it writes affine C5, not projective P5.
Reconstruction. Genus two, rank two, fixed odd determinant, stability, and projec-
tive ambient space are all candidate reconstruction, not recovered words. A general
hyperelliptic curve is not itself a three-dimensional intersection of two quadrics. An
affine chart of the classical moduli threefold could explain C5, but no chart is spec-
ified.
Fate. Ramanan (1980, §10, p. 546) states the hyperelliptic moduli/quadric con-
struction and explicitly attributes the genus-two case to Newstead (1968) and
Narasimhan–Ramanan (1969); the publisher metadata for Narasimhan and Ra-
manan (1969) was also checked. This is a strong historical candidate, not an exact
identification of the under-specified arrow.
Present status. As of November 2026, the candidate classical theorem is settled,
but the precise object intended by this notebook shorthand remains uncertain.
Assessment. literal statement: not determinable; publication match: thematic only;
verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: medium

1976-410.10
Nodal spectral curves and soliton solutions
Class A
Kind; source. method; P000682
The notes. The notes describe a soliton as a nonstationary solution obtained by
using a stationary spectral curve with one double point and distinct tangents.
Statement. In the algebro-geometric soliton construction, a rational spectral curve
with an ordinary node is obtained by identifying two distinct smooth points of its
normalization. A spectral line bundle, or higher-rank bundle with specified gluing
matrix, together with marked-point asymptotics and compatible commuting con-
nections, can produce one-soliton-type solutions. More nodes give corresponding
gluing parameters and multisoliton families. The node alone does not determine a
solution or ensure real nonsingularity.
Reconstruction. The normalization, gluing, spectral data and compatibility assump-
tions are supplied to replace the overbroad descriptive definition. The actual double
point in the note has two branches; a cusp would require a different local construc-
tion.
Fate. Manin (1979, Chapter III, §1.4; §5, especially §§5.1–5.5 and 5.12; §6.1) gives
the precise nodal gluing construction and identifies the number of pairs of glued
points with the soliton count. The rank-one degeneration was older; this class A
entry concerns the rank-two/matrix construction being explained by the speaker
and published in his 1978 survey.
Present status. As of November 2026, the stated spectral-data construction is es-
tablished; the literal node-only criterion needs the additional data and admissibility
conditions.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
partial; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high
1976-410.11
Differential-operator algebra and compatible
evolution conventions
Class A
Kind; source. definition; P000682
The notes. The algebraic setting is a characteristic-zero field k, a k-algebra B, and
commuting derivations ∂, ∂1, ∂2. The analytic example is Mℓ(B0), with meromorphic
coefficients. The notebook lists stationary Lax, nonstationary Lax and Zakharov–
Shabat equations.
Statement. Let B be a unital associative central k-algebra with commuting k-
derivations d, d1, d2.
The Ore algebra B[D; d] consists of finite sums P bjDj, with
multiplication fixed by Db = bD + d(b).
The derivations di extend coefficientwise
and satisfy diD = 0. Stationarity is [L, P] = 0. With the later connection convention
∇11 = P1, ∇21 = L1, the compatible equations are d1L = [P, L] and d1L −d2P = [P, L].
Reconstruction. The Ore multiplication rule is made explicit, especially because
matrix coefficients need not commute. The notebook’s initial d1L = [L, P] is another
admissible convention after replacing the evolution operator by its negative, but
it cannot be mixed unchanged with the later positive connection definitions. The
derivative-plus sign in its third equation is corrected in the final item.
Fate. The algebraic setup is exactly Manin (1979, Chapter III, §2.1, p. 81); the
connections are treated in Theorems 2.5–2.6. This packages older Lax and zero-
curvature equations into the speaker’s subsequent bimodule exposition.
Present status. As of November 2026, the algebra and the convention-dependent
equations are established; a consistent choice of signs is necessary.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
exact; verification: read the result; development: annotated; confidence: high

1976-410.12
Krichever–Drinfeld filtered bimodules
Class A
Kind; source. definition; P000682-P000683
The notes. The structures are a left B-module and right O-module M, a distinguished
1, an exhaustive filtration, and a connection ∇. The actions are faithful and com-
mute; M−1 = 0, M0 = B1, and every graded piece has rank one. The final line gives
i ≥−1 for both the filtration inclusion and the induced isomorphism.
Statement. Take O to be the coordinate ring of a geometrically integral affine curve
over k. Require commuting faithful left B and right O actions, an exhaustive filtra-
tion by left submodules 0 = M−1 ⊂M0 ⊂· · ·, with 1 a free generator of M0, and each
Mi/Mi−1 free of rank one for i ≥0. Require ∇(bm) = d(b)m + b∇m, ∇(mϕ) = (∇m)ϕ,
∇Mi ⊂Mi+1, and induced isomorphisms Mi/Mi−1 →Mi+1/Mi for i ≥0.
Reconstruction. The induced maps on graded pieces are B-linear because the extra
Leibniz term drops to the lower filtration level. The isomorphism cannot start at
i = −1: its source would be zero (or involve an undefined M−2) while its target
is M0 ̸= 0.
The degree-raising inclusion alone is harmless at −1.
Earlier-index
continuity: 1975-385.1. These links identify a recurring statement or construction;
only the explicitly indexed inverse problem reuses a problem ID.
Fate. These are the structures and axioms of Manin (1979, Chapter III, §2.2, p. 81),
a close publication match to the notebook. The induced-isomorphism range is in-
terpreted as i ≥0; no nonexistent initial graded isomorphism is retained.
Present status. As of November 2026, the corrected axioms give the filtered
differential-module model used in the reconstruction theorem.
Assessment. literal statement: false as written; publication match: exact; verifica-
tion: read the result; development: annotated; confidence: high
1976-410.13
The regular bimodule attached to commuting
operators
Class A
Kind; source. construction; P000683
The notes. For commuting L, P, with ¯k[L, P] having no zero divisors, the notes put

∇Q = DQ.
Statement. Given an injective k-algebra homomorphism i : O ,→B[D; d], where O
is the coordinate ring of a geometrically integral affine curve, take M = B[D; d],

j=0 bjDj}, and
∇Q = DQ. These satisfy all the preceding axioms.
Reconstruction. For the special choice O = k[L, P], one must also ensure that it is

arbitrary matrix operators.
Fate. This is exactly Manin (1979, Chapter III, §2.2, p. 81), which starts from an em-

1976-410.14

Class A

The notes. The first theorem recovers an embedding O →B[D] from the bimodule.
The diagrams first identify differential operators generated by B and ∇, then define
Lφ1 = 1ϕ.
Statement. For a bimodule satisfying the corrected axioms above, the map B[D; d] →

extra unrecorded geometric correspondence is needed. Earlier-index continuity:

1976-410.15
A second connection produces Lax evolution
Class A
Kind; source. theorem; P000683
The notes. The second theorem introduces ∇1 commuting with ∇and the right O-

This fixes the sign for the stated convention.

1976-410.16
Three commuting connections and the shared
zero-curvature sign error
Class A
Kind; source. theorem; P000684
The notes. The third theorem drops all mention of O, assumes pairwise commuting
∇, ∇1, ∇2, puts ∇11 = P1, ∇21 = L1, and writes ∂2P + ∂1L = ±[L, P]. Both the plus
sign and the explicit sign uncertainty are visible in the scan.


===== PDF PAGE 60 =====
CHAPTER 1. THE ACADEMIC YEARS 1976–77 AND 1977–78
60
Statement. Let the filtered left B-module, cyclic element and d-connection satisfy the
preceding axioms with O omitted. Let d, d1, d2 commute and let their connections
∇, ∇1, ∇2 commute pairwise. Define P, L by the notebook’s two equations. Then
d1L −d2P = [P, L].
Equivalently, [d1−P, d2−L] = 0 as differential operators acting on a common auxiliary
wavefunction.
Reconstruction. On the cyclic vector, flatness gives 0 = (d1L+LP −d2P −PL)1. Thus
the derivative terms have opposite signs, and the bracket sign is fixed. As a concrete
check, take B = k[t, z], d = 0, M = B[D], ∇= D, ∇1 = ∂t + Rz, ∇2 = ∂z + Rt, where R is
right multiplication. These connections commute and give P = z, L = t; the correct
difference is zero, whereas the notebook’s sum is 2 and either commutator sign is
zero.
Fate. Manin (1979, Chapter III, Theorem 2.6, p. 83) is an exact structural match, but
its printed English statement also has a plus sign: with its swapped labels ∇11 = L1,
∇21 = P1, it prints d1P + d2L = [P, L]. Its own flatness proof requires d1P −d2L =
[L, P]. The scan of that printed page was inspected. Thus publication confirms the
theorem’s provenance but does not validate the literal formula; this is a faithful
error shared by the notebook and the cited translation.
Present status. As of November 2026, the corrected flatness identity is established;
the literal plus-sign formula is false even for scalar commuting multiplication oper-
ators.
Assessment. literal statement: false as written; publication match: exact; verifica-
tion: read the result; development: annotated; confidence: high
1.15
J. J. Duistermaat, 1977-02-21: Morse indices,
Lagrangian intersections, and the spherical trace
formula
record 411; source pages P000685-P000689.
The first part explains the finite Morse index of a regular variational problem with
general endpoint constraints through a path of Lagrangian subspaces. The general-
boundary theory was publicly presented in May 1974. The second part passes from
spherical convolution to a distributional Selberg formula and joint spectral asymp-
totics; its identifiable continuation is the 1979 paper with Kolk and Varadarajan. The
notebook suppresses transversality and normalization conventions, indexes orbital
terms by elements rather than conjugacy classes, and ends with a remainder expo-
nent not supported by the matched theorem. These are assessed separately.

1.16
Yu. I. Manin, 1977-02-28: Filtered bimodules, nodal
spectral curves, and soliton equations
record 412; source pages P000690-P000693.
This continuation recapitulates the bimodule reconstruction of commuting opera-
tors, Lax evolutions, and zero-curvature equations, then constructs the modules ex-
plicitly on an affine line with pairs of points identified. The block interpolation matrix,
filtration, connection matrices, and operator orders closely match Chapter III, sec-
tions 2 and 5, of Manin’s 1978 Russian exposition (English translation 1979). Several
signs and indices in the notebook are demonstrably inconsistent with that construc-
tion. The final question about the behaviour of an N-soliton solution specifies neither
an equation nor a limiting regime; it is retained without manufacturing a new open
problem.

The notes. The coefficients are B = Mℓ(B0), where B0 consists of meromorphic germs

1.17
A. M. Gabrielov, 1977-03-14: Pontryagin cycles,
changes of sections, and hypersimplicial chains
record 413; source pages P000694-P000696.
Gelfand motivates a combinatorial theory by local Euler-characteristic formulas
and the difficulty of noncommuting rotations. Gabrielov then describes the first Pon-
tryagin class by a corank-two degeneracy locus, reduces the chain calculations to pairs
of sections of a real plane bundle, and organizes their cobordisms by hypersimplices.
The notes identify MacPherson’s February 1977 Bourbaki exposition, which was pub-
lished in the 1978 proceedings.
The core constructions were already in the two
Gabrielov–Gelfand–Losik papers of 1975. The final diagrams sketch low-dimensional
chain assignments rather than a new, fully specified open problem.

The programme of an elementary geometry of

Class F

The notes. Gelfand says that an elementary geometry of higher-dimensional polyhe-

1976-414.4
A wavefront-set heuristic from singular plane
integrals
Class F
Kind; source. method; P000697
The notes. The page suggests that (x0, ξ0) ∈WF(u) when integration over parallel
planes in direction ξ0 has a singularity.
Statement. For a localized distribution, Radon data can detect conormal singulari-
ties through the incidence canonical relation. An unlocalized integral over a whole
plane does not identify which point caused a singularity, and cancellations can erase
contributions. Thus a pointwise criterion requires localization near x0, a convention
making ξ0 a normal covector, and a precise microlocal implication.

notebook. No unique equivalence is reconstructed from the single sentence.
Fate. The related characteristic geometry is explained in Guillemin (1985, §§2–3);
the notebook’s particular wavefront criterion is not matched.

I. M. Gelfand, 1977-03-28: Poncelet closure and

The short intervention above Vershik’s heading states the Poncelet porism for two

1976-416.1
When does the Poncelet polygon close?
Class E
Kind; source. problem; P000699
The notes. A polygon is inscribed in one conic and tangent to another; Gelfand asks
when such a polygon closes, citing Griffiths.
Statement. For two nonsingular complex plane conics whose incidence curve of a
point on the outer conic and a tangent to the inner conic is smooth, that curve is
genus one. The successive-tangent operation is translation by a fixed δ on it. A
polygon closes after m steps exactly when mδ = 0; exact period m requires δ to have
exact order m. Nested real ovals select the real branch and direction of iteration.
Reconstruction. Smoothness of the incidence curve excludes degenerate conic
pairs. The elliptic-curve translation is the precise reconstruction behind the notes’
mention of Jacobi’s elliptic integrals.
Fate. See Griffiths (1976, §I(d), pp. 345–348), published before the talk.
Present status. As of November 2026: the closure criterion is classical and solved.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
exact; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high; problem
GP-1976-23


===== PDF PAGE 83 =====

1976-416.2
Poncelet’s porism
Class E
Kind; source. theorem; P000699
The notes. If the construction closes from one point, it closes from every other point
in the same number of steps.
Statement. For the nondegenerate conic pair and consistent tangent iteration
above, the existence of a closed m-step orbit means the elliptic translation is m-
torsion. Therefore its mth iterate is the identity for every admissible starting point.
Exceptional points of the algebraic construction are understood by extension.
Reconstruction. The same branch and a nondegenerate conic pair are required;
changing tangent directions arbitrarily is not this map.
Fate. The porism is stated in Griffiths (1976, §I(d), formula (1.37) and discussion).
Present status. As of November 2026: the theorem is classical.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
exact; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high; problem

Invariant measure and circle-rotation coordinates
Class E
Kind; source. claim; P000699
The notes. The notebook writes that the Poncelet theorem implies an invariant mea-
sure and an isomorphism with a circle rotation.
Statement. For nested smooth real conics with a regular real Poncelet map, the
elliptic-curve parameter gives an invariant positive measure on the relevant circle
component. Integrating and normalizing this measure gives a coordinate conjugat-
ing the map to a rigid rotation. Rational rotation number is the closure criterion.
Reconstruction. The construction uses the elliptic geometry and a full-support
nonatomic invariant density.
The mere logical statement of a porism does not
supply such a density or the conjugacy.
Fate. The elliptic-parameter construction is in Griffiths (1976, §I(d)).
Present status. As of November 2026: the geometric rotation description is estab-
lished, with the stated regularity.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
partial; verification: read the result; development: annotated; confidence: high

A. M. Vershik, 1977-03-28: Probability, the Young
graph, and Plancherel asymptotics
record 415; source pages P000699-P000701.
The manuscript starts Vershik’s talk below Gelfand’s intervention on the page
headed 28 March 1977, without another date. The catalogue’s 4 April date is therefore
corrected here, with the catalogue value preserved in the provenance. The exposition
moves from Young’s finite representation theory and branching paths to Plancherel
growth, the limit shape, Ulam’s longest increasing subsequence problem, and a pro-
posed fluctuation theorem. The exact limit-shape and first-row results are in the 1977
Vershik–Kerov announcement, received in December 1976. The fluctuation line is not
an exact statement of the later Kerov theorem: both the displayed fourth-root scaling
and the white-noise description require correction.

1976-415.18
Ulam’s problem for a random real sequence
Class A
Kind; source. question; P000701
The notes. For uniform xi
∈[0, 1], the notes ask for the greatest increasing-
subsequence length and state 2√n.
The displayed maximum is over r, but the
subsequence indices end at n.
Statement. Let X1, . . . , Xn be independent uniform random variables on [0, 1], and
let Ln = max{r : ∃i1 < · · · < ir, Xi1 < · · · < Xir}. Then Ln/(2√n) →1 in probability.
The same holds for any independent identically distributed continuous law, since
its ranks form a uniform permutation.
Reconstruction. Independence, continuity, and the terminal index r are supplied.
With continuous laws, strict and weak inequalities agree almost surely.
Fate. The uniform version is Vershik and Kerov (1977, Corollary 2, p. 1027); the
rank reduction uses Schensted (1961, Theorems 1 and 3).
Present status. As of November 2026: the first-order Ulam problem is solved.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:

1976-417.3
The flat bundle associated to a representation
Class E
Kind; source. construction; P000702
The notes. The notes form Eρ = U(X) × V n/π1(X) and declare two representations
analytically equivalent when their underlying bundles are isomorphic.
Statement. For a representation ρ : π1(X) →GLn(C), quotient the trivial bundle on

1976-417.4
A packet is a fiber of forgetting the flat structure
Class E
Kind; source. definition; P000702
The notes. For fixed X, E, a packet H(X, E) is a subset of the representation space.
Statement. H(X, E) consists of conjugacy classes of representations whose asso-
ciated holomorphic bundle is isomorphic to E. Equivalently, it is the set of holo-
morphic connections on E, modulo the action of Authol(E). On a complex curve a
holomorphic connection is flat because its holomorphic curvature is a two-form.
Reconstruction. The quotient by bundle automorphisms is necessary before calling
this a space of representation classes.
Fate. See Tyurin (1978, Ch. II, Definition 2.2.8 and Exercises 1–5 to §2, pp. 199,

1976-417.7
Any two irreducibles need not become analytically
equivalent
Class E
Kind; source. claim; P000702
The notes. The manuscript states Любые два неприв. предст. эквивал. относит.
нек-рой рим. пов. and explains that they enter one packet.
Statement. The literal assertion is false. Fix g ≥2, 0 < θ < 2π, and rank-one unitary
characters χ0 = 1, χ1(a1) = eiθ, with χ1 = 1 on the remaining generators. Both are
irreducible and satisfy the surface relation, but for every complex structure on Σg
their underlying holomorphic line bundles are nonisomorphic.
Reconstruction. Proof: an isomorphism would give a nowhere-zero holomorphic
section of the unitary flat line bundle with character χ1χ−1
0 . In unitary parallel lo-
cal frames, its log norm is harmonic; compactness makes it constant. Each local
holomorphic coefficient then has constant modulus, hence is constant. The sec-
tion is parallel, forcing the character to be trivial, a contradiction. An independent
universal-cover derivation reaches the same conclusion. This is a correction of a
false literal assertion, not a new open problem.
Fate. Tyurin (1978, Ch. II, §2, Exercise 10, p. 204) states that a packet cannot con-
tain two distinct unitary representation classes. The 1973-lecture provenance es-
tablishes that the surrounding theory was not new in the 1978 printing; no claim is
made that this exact notebook sentence is reproduced in the publication.
Present status. As of November 2026: the universal assertion is disproved by the
checked rank-one example.
Assessment. literal statement: false as written; publication match: partial; verifi-
cation: read the result; development: annotated; confidence: high
1976-417.8
The affine fibration exists only over an appropriate
bundle locus
Class E
Kind; source. claim; P000702
The notes. The notes draw RC →Sn(X), label it an affine bundle, and call Sn(X) the
moduli of bundles.
Statement. Let X be a smooth projective complex curve of genus g ≥2. Over the
moduli of stable degree-zero bundles, forgetting a holomorphic connection gives a
torsor modeled on the cotangent bundle, with fiber dimension n2(g −1) + 1; fixing
determinant and its connection gives the trace-free version. The whole character
variety does not map to stable-bundle moduli in this way: an irreducible flat bundle
can have unstable underlying holomorphic bundle.
Reconstruction. This is a restricted de Rham moduli statement. In a presentation
using the Betti character variety, Riemann–Hilbert is analytic, so the notebook does
not establish an algebraic affine bundle on the full Betti quotient.
Fate. The difference-of-connections model is Tyurin (1978, Ch. II, §2, Exercises 1–5);
the moduli-locus and torsor formulation make the suppressed hypotheses explicit.
Present status. As of November 2026: the restricted torsor picture is standard; the
global unrestricted diagram is too strong.

1976-417.15
A marked character packet determines the curve
through Torelli
Class E
Kind; source. theorem; P000703
The notes. The notes claim that the surface can be recovered uniquely from a
packet, by a theorem written as Terelli.
Statement. Regard a nonempty packet as its embedded analytic subset in the char-
acter torus of the fixed oriented marked surface. Translating it to the identity gives
the trivial packet; its tangent space identifies H1,0(X) ⊂H1(Σg, C). Together with the
integral lattice and intersection pairing this gives the principally polarized Jacobian,
which determines X up to isomorphism by Torelli.
Reconstruction. The embedding, integral marking, and polarization are indispens-
able. An abstract affine space of dimension g, or an unpolarized torus without the
retained data, is not the claimed invariant.
Fate. This is the content of Tyurin (1978, Ch. II, §2, Exercises 12–13, pp. 204–205).
Present status. As of November 2026: the Torelli conclusion is valid with the re-
tained marking and polarization.

1976-417.18
Schwarzians require a projective atlas to become
quadratic differentials
Class E
Kind; source. claim; P000703
The notes. After the chain rule, the notes say that general quadratic differentials
result.
Statement. Local Schwarzians transform as a projective connection under arbitrary
holomorphic coordinate changes. After choosing a projective atlas, whose transi-
tion functions are Möbius, the extra Schwarzian term vanishes and the resulting
expressions can be compared as quadratic differentials. The difference of two pro-
jective connections is intrinsically a quadratic differential.
Reconstruction. Choosing the projective structure is essential; the notebook sup-
presses that choice.
Fate. The projective-atlas assumption is explicit in Tyurin (1977, Introduction and
Definition 1.2).
Present status. As of November 2026: the claim is correct with the projective-
coordinate hypothesis.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:

1.22
Catalogue heading for 1977–78 and adjacent
next-year context
record 418; source pages P000705-P000706.
This catalogue record marks the 1977–78 academic-year boundary; it is retained
as a heading, with no fabricated 1976–77 mathematical occurrences. On P000705
the source starts the seminar’s thirty-fourth year and proposes noncommutative al-
gebraic geometry in connection with Bernstein, I. M. Gelfand and S. I. Gelfand. A
distinct heading then names V. Drinfeld and Atiyah’s work on Yang–Mills equations.
P000705–P000706 contain the beginning of that adjacent talk: self-dual curvature
on S4, conformal invariance of the Hodge star on two-forms, a heuristic quantiza-
tion/determinant discussion, the twistor map CP3 →HP1 = S4 with fibers CP1, and a
correspondence with bundles trivial on twistor fibers, followed by the quaternionic
real structure.
These adjacent contents are inventoried for coverage and must be
catalogued with the next academic year. The visible date is transcribed as 5/IV-77,
in tension with the boundary; the exact month is left unresolved rather than silently
changed to September.

1.23
S. I. Gelfand, 1978-02-13: Exterior-algebra modules
and algebraic bundles (manuscript: 13 February
1978)
record 437; source pages P000753-P000756.
Provenance correction. The supplied catalogue date, 13 February 1977, is cor-
rected in the structured date field to the manuscript date, 13 February 1978; the
catalogue value is preserved here and in the provenance ledger. The scan of P000753
unambiguously reads 13/II–78 г.; P000757 begins a new lecture, 27/II–78 г.. Thus
this is a 1977–78 talk accidentally included in the requested package, not evidence
for a 1976–77 announcement. The occurrence prefix 1977-437 follows the format rule
that YEAR is the first year of the actual academic year. The requested record is re-
tained in this package as an explicitly identified chronological appendix. P000752
is an adjacent physics tail; P000757–P000758 belong to the next, separately dated,
S. I. Gelfand lecture. They are accounted for in the source ledger and corrections,
without assigning their new content to record 437.
The talk explains how finite graded modules over an exterior algebra produce com-
plexes on projective space. A fibrewise exactness condition singles out vector bun-
dles; graded free summands disappear, and every bundle is represented uniquely
up to stable module isomorphism. Twisting is expressed through a cosyzygy and a
grading shift, followed by an outline of the inverse construction from large-twist sec-
tions. I. M. Gelfand calls for coherent sheaves and derived categories, and Serre’s
projective module–sheaf correspondence closes the surviving lecture. The exact pub-
lication is Bernstein et al. (1978, Theorem 1 and its remarks): received 10 January
1978, before the manuscript’s lecture date, published in the 1978 Russian volume;
the English translation bears a 1979 copyright imprint. A submission date is not, by
itself, a verified date of public circulation. Class A here denotes subsequent publica-
tion of the current work; it does not assert discovery after the lecture. The modern
derived equivalence is given there by Theorem 2, but is not silently attributed to the
surviving lecture as a fully stated theorem.
1977-437.1
Topoi and derived categories as organizing concepts
Class E
Kind; source. remark; P000753; I. M. Gelfand
The notes. I. M. Gelfand identifies Топос and Производная категория as two pri-
mary concepts of category theory.
Statement. This is a pedagogical judgement, not a theorem that category theory
has precisely two basic notions. A Grothendieck topos is a category equivalent to
sheaves of sets on a site; for an abelian category A, its derived category inverts
quasi-isomorphisms of complexes.
Reconstruction. The definitions clarify the two names; the evaluative word primary
remains the speaker’s judgement.
Fate. The frameworks predate this lecture:
Artin et al. (1972, Exposé IV) and
Hartshorne (1966, Chapter I).
Present status. As of 10 November 2026: Both frameworks remain established; the
notebook supplies no testable exclusivity claim.

1977-437.12
Uniqueness is stable graded isomorphism
Class A
Kind; source. theorem; P000754-P000755
The notes. Theorem 1′(2) states Φ(V ) ∼= Φ(V ′) iff V ⊕P ∼= V ′ ⊕P ′ for free P, P ′.
Statement. For two finite-dimensional graded proper Λ-modules, their associated
bundles are isomorphic exactly when there exist finite graded free modules P, P ′
with a degree-zero Λ-module isomorphism V ⊕P ≃V ′ ⊕P ′. The two added free
modules need not coincide.
Reconstruction. The grading and the properness of both modules are carried over
from the preceding definition.
Fate. Exactly the uniqueness clause of Bernstein et al. (1978, Theorem 1).
Present status. As of 10 November 2026: Established. The stronger categorical
form quotients morphisms factoring through graded free modules.
Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: exact; verification: read
the result; development: annotated; confidence: high; problem GP-1977-01

i xif(ξiλ); it preserves internal degree. The tail with

1977-437.20
Vector bundles must be enlarged to coherent
sheaves
Class E
Kind; source. remark; P000756; I. M. Gelfand
The notes. I. M. Gelfand says classification of bundles is not quite the right problem
because they do not form an abelian category; one must allow fibre dimensions to
vary and introduce sheaves.
Statement. For n ≥1, finite-rank locally free sheaves on Pn are not an abelian cat-
egory with the inherited sheaf morphisms: multiplication by a nonzero linear form
gives O(−1) →O whose sheaf cokernel is the structure sheaf of a hyperplane and
is not locally free. Coherent sheaves form an abelian category. For n = 0, vector
bundles are finite-dimensional vector spaces and already form an abelian category.
Reconstruction. More intrinsically, that multiplication map is monic and epic within
the category of locally free sheaves, since a map from its torsion cokernel to a locally
free sheaf is zero, but is not an isomorphism on the hyperplane. An abelian category
is balanced, so it cannot have such a map. The point case is exceptional.
Fate. Coherent-sheaf formalism predates the lecture: Serre (1955, Chapter I, §2).
Present status. As of 10 November 2026: Correct with the positive-dimensional
qualification; variable fibre rank alone is not a definition of a coherent sheaf.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
exact; verification: read the result; development: annotated; confidence: high
1977-437.21
Serre correspondence is a quotient by
finite-dimensional graded modules
Class E

The notes. The notes equate sheaves on CP n with finitely generated graded
C[x0, . . . , xn]-modules up to finitely many homogeneous components.
Statement. For S = C[x0, . . . , xn] with standard grading, sheafification induces an
equivalence grmod(S)/ fdim(S) ≃Coh(Pn
C). Here the numerator has finitely gener-
ated graded modules and degree-zero maps, and the denominator is its Serre sub-
category of finite-dimensional modules. A quasi-inverse is the nonnegative graded
section module followed by passage to the quotient.
Reconstruction. A quotient category replaces the informal phrase ignoring finitely
many components; coherence is an essential omitted hypothesis.
Fate. Serre (1955, Chapter III, §§57–65); The Stacks Project Authors (2026b, Propo-

Example 1.3 (A limitation of the density criterion). [proved here] The analytic equa-
tion y′′ = y3 has the solution y = 0, zero constant and linear transverse terms, and zero
quadratic transverse coefficient. Thus the density constructed along this particular
solution vanishes. Nevertheless, the classical relative invariant is H = 6Fyy = 36y,
which is a nonzero formal series. The equation cannot be formally point-flat. This
checks the literal one-solution reading of P000666; it does not rule out a stronger in-
tended statement with additional quantifiers and hypotheses. Direct substitution into
the complete H-formula and the shorter Fp = 0 calculation agree.

1.24.2
Critical checks on the Berezinian and flatness signs
These calculations audit faithful mathematical errors in the source. They are elemen-
tary critical annotations, not a claim to new research and not a development of a class
C or D problem.
The reciprocal Berezinian on P000673.
[computed here] For an even block-
diagonal matrix J = diag(a, d) of size 1|1, with nonzero even scalar entries, the usual
Berezinian is Ber J = a/d. The first expression on P000673 gives d/a, whereas the
lower expression on the same page gives a/d. Taking a = 2, d = 3 gives 3/2 versus 2/3,
so the two expressions cannot be simultaneous definitions in the stated convention.
The usual normalization is explicitly printed in Bernstein and Leites (1977, §1) and
in the earlier density formula (Berezin and Leites, 1975, formula (3)). The correct
general formula is
Ber
A
B
C
D

= det(A −BD−1C)
det D
=
det A
det(D −CA−1B).
This test isolates a reciprocal error without depending on conventions for left versus
right odd derivatives.
Flatness on P000684 and in the printed Manin theorem.
[proved here] Use the
notebook’s convention ∇11 = P1 and ∇21 = L1. Because both connections commute
with ∇, the coefficientwise Leibniz rule gives
[∇1, ∇2]1 = (d1L + LP −d2P −PL)1.
The cyclic filtered-module theorem makes the action on 1 faithful for differential op-
erators, hence flatness implies
d1L −d2P = [P, L].
Both the difference sign and the order of the commutator follow from the declared
convention. An unspecified ± in front of a commutator cannot repair a sum of the two
derivatives.
For a concrete counterexample to that sum, take B = Q[t, z], d = 0, M = B[D], and
∇Q = DQ,
∇1Q = ∂tQ + Qz,
∇2Q = ∂zQ + Qt.
The standard order filtration satisfies the module axioms; the three connections com-
mute. Their values on 1 are P = z and L = t. Thus d1L−d2P = 1−1 = 0 = [P, L], whereas
d1L + d2P = 2 ̸= 0. The accompanying exact symbolic check verifies this on a general
test polynomial in t, z, D.
The error is also present in the inspected printed English statement (Manin, 1979,
Chapter III, Theorem 2.6, p. 83). That statement uses the opposite names, ∇11 = L1,
∇21 = P1, and prints d1P +d2L = [P, L]. Its proof requires instead d1P −d2L = [L, P]. This
identifies a shared mathematical sign error, not a failure to transcribe the notebook.


===== PDF PAGE 111 =====
CHAPTER 1. THE ACADEMIC YEARS 1976–77 AND 1977–78
111

[computed here] For P000681, the companion’s
separate critical_checks.py computes the commutator by two methods.
With L =
D4 + vD2 + wD + z, P = D2 + AD + v/2, and Ax = 0, its coefficients are
[L, P]D2 = 2vxx −Avx −2wx,
[L, P]D = 2vxxx + vvx −wxx −Awx −2zx,
[L, P]1 = 1

2vvxx + 1
2wvx −zxx −Azx.
The notebook’s first two relative coefficients are therefore wrong, while the third
equation agrees. Manin’s later equation has the corrected relative coefficients, with
its own time and commutator conventions (Manin, 1979, Chapter III, §6.3, equa-
tion (17), p. 96).
1.24.3
Audit of the assertion that changing the complex structure
merges any two packets
The notebook, P000702, third line from the bottom before the final diagram, says
Любые два неприв. предст. эквивал. относит. нек-рой рим. пов. (вх. в какой-то
пакет). The scan confirms the two universal quantifiers; the word abbreviated as
неприв. is overwritten but is the diplomatic transcription’s reading. The definition
earlier on the same page makes equivalence mean Eρ1 ∼= Eρ2 as holomorphic bundles.
The assertion therefore includes the following rank-one case, in which it is false. The
author’s own survey explicitly gives uniqueness of the unitary representation in a
packet as an exercise (Tyurin, 1978, Ch. II, §2, Exercise 10, p. 204). This is an audit
of a literal assertion, not the development of a C or D problem.
Proposition 1.7 (Distinct unitary characters remain in distinct packets). [proved
here] Let Σ be a connected, closed, oriented surface of genus g ≥1, and let χ1, χ2 :
π1(Σ) →U(1) be two distinct characters. For every complex structure J compatible
with the orientation, the holomorphic line bundles obtained from the two unitary flat
bundles on X = (Σ, J) are nonisomorphic. In particular, the notebook’s assertion about
arbitrary pairs of irreducible representations is false.
Proof. Write Li for the flat line bundle with monodromy χi, with its parallel Hermi-
tian metric and holomorphic structure given by the (0, 1)-part of the flat connection.
Suppose that Φ : L1 →L2 is a holomorphic bundle isomorphism. It is a nowhere-zero
holomorphic section s of the unitary flat line bundle L = L2 ⊗L−1
1 , whose monodromy
is δ = χ2χ−1
1 .
On any simply connected coordinate neighbourhood choose a parallel frame e of
norm one for L. Because the (0, 1)-connection defines the holomorphic structure, e is
holomorphic. Thus s = fe with f holomorphic and nowhere zero. The real function u =
log∥s∥= log |f| is harmonic on that neighbourhood. Unitary transition functions make
u a globally defined harmonic function on the compact connected Riemann surface
X; hence u is constant. Each holomorphic function f therefore has constant modulus
and is constant. Consequently s is a nonzero parallel section of L. Parallel transport
around every loop fixes its nonzero value, so δ = 1 and χ1 = χ2, a contradiction.
For an explicit pair, use the presentation in the notebook. Take χ1 = 1, let χ2(a1) =
eiθ for θ /∈2πZ, and send every other standard generator to 1. The surface relation
holds because U(1) is abelian. Both are one-dimensional complex representations and
therefore irreducible. The argument applies to every compatible J, including genus
g ≥2 if that was the intended range. Even allowing a change of marking cannot turn
the nontrivial character into the trivial character.


===== PDF PAGE 112 =====
CHAPTER 1. THE ACADEMIC YEARS 1976–77 AND 1977–78
112
Remark 1.8 (Independent check). The proof was checked again by working on the
universal cover. A holomorphic isomorphism there is multiplication by a nowhere-
zero holomorphic function F with F(γu) = δ(γ)F(u). Since |δ(γ)| = 1, log |F| descends to
a harmonic function on X, hence is constant; the open mapping theorem then makes
F constant and forces δ = 1. This check uses neither the higher-rank stability theorem
nor an assumption that all flat holomorphic bundles are stable.
1.24.4
Critical checks for the miscatalogued exterior-algebra lecture
These checks concern record 437, whose manuscript date is 13 February 1978. They
audit the supplied material without turning it into evidence of a 1976–77 research
problem. The results are elementary verifications of the construction recorded in the
notebook; no claim of novelty is intended.
Proposition 1.9 (The regular exterior module is acyclic). [proved here] Let k be a
field, let Ξ have finite dimension m ≥1, and let 0 ̸= ξ ∈Ξ. Give Λ = V Ξ its negative
grading and differential d(v) = ξ ∧v. Then the complex is contractible. Consequently
every finite direct sum of grading shifts of Λ gives the zero bundle under the construc-
tion of record 437.
Proof. Choose a ∈Ξ∗with a(ξ) = 1. Define contraction on a simple exterior tensor by
ιa(v1 ∧· · · ∧vr) =
r
X
j=1
(−1)j−1a(vj)v1 ∧· · · ∧bvj ∧· · · ∧vr.
Applying this formula to ξ ∧v, the term contracting the first factor is v; each remaining
term is the negative of the corresponding term of ξ∧ιa(v). Therefore ιad+dιa = 1. If dv =
0, this identity gives v = dιa(v), so every cycle is a boundary. The homotopy commutes
with taking direct sums and does not depend on the choice of grading origin.
In
particular the output has rank zero, which resolves the notebook’s ambiguous use of
“trivial” in this example.
Remark 1.10 (Independent checks). [computed here] The identity was independently
re-derived by writing Ξ = kξ ⊕U, hence Λ = V U ⊕ξ ∧V U. The differential maps the
first summand isomorphically to the second and kills the second. The exact-arithmetic
program code/critical_checks.py additionally constructs exterior multiplication and
contraction by two separate bit-mask formulas. For m = 1, . . . , 6 and three nonzero
vectors in each dimension, all 18 cases satisfy d2 = 0, ιad + dιa = 1, and zero homology
in every degree. The ranks agree with
 m−1
r

in exterior degree r. These finite checks
support the implementation; the preceding proof establishes the assertion for every
m, not an extrapolation from the computed cases.
Example 1.11 (The inverse construction requires a grading convention). [proved
here] Take n = 0, so the base is a point, and let E = O. For every N ≥0, the two
consecutive section spaces are one-dimensional and X0 is the identity. If the two ten-
sor factors in P000756 are given their raw exterior grading, then
∂: Λ −→Λ,
∂(1) = ξ0,
∂(ξ0) = 0.
Its kernel is kξ0, concentrated in internal degree −1. The action of ξ0 on that kernel is
zero, so its fibre homology is nonzero in degree −1; the raw kernel is not proper. This
is an explicit check of an omitted grading convention, not a counterexample to the
BGG correspondence. The dual free module Homk(Λ, k) differs from Λ by a grading
shift, which is essential in the precise inverse construction (Bernstein et al., 1978, §4).


===== PDF PAGE 113 =====
CHAPTER 1. THE ACADEMIC YEARS 1976–77 AND 1977–78
113
Proposition 1.12 (Sign of the twist operation). [proved here] Use the negative grad-
ing of record 437. If V is proper and 0 →V →P →V ′ →0 is an exact sequence of
finite graded exterior-algebra modules with P free, set Wj = V ′
j+1. Then W is proper
and Φ(W) = Φ(V )(1).
Proof. For each nonzero ξ, applying Lξ gives a short exact sequence of complexes.
The middle complex is acyclic by the proposition above, so its homology sequence
gives Hj(Lξ(V ′)) ∼= Hj−1(Lξ(V )). Consequently the only possibly nonzero homology of
V ′ is in chain degree one, and the displayed grading shift moves it to degree zero. On
projective space,
Lj(W) = V ′
1−j ⊗O(j) = Lj−1(V ′)(1).
The short exact sequence of sheaf complexes gives H−1(L(V ′)) ∼= H0(L(V )). Reindexing
by the displayed formula proves the claimed twist. Depending on the convention for
a shifted complex, a minus sign in its differential is removed by multiplying degree j
by (−1)j; cohomology and the bundle are unaffected.
Remark 1.13 (Second check of indexing). There are two independent index tests: the
chain homology sequence places the cokernel’s fibre homology in degree one, whereas
the sheaf cohomology sequence places it in degree minus one. The substitution j 7→−j
in Lj(V ) = V−j ⊗O(j) reconciles them and gives O(1), not O(−1). This is exactly the
convention in remark 2 after Theorem 1 of Bernstein et al. (1978).
Remark 1.14 (Independent check of the inverse-module indexing). [proved here] The
conventions of occurrence 1977-437.19 were checked against Eisenbud et al. (2003,
§2, Corollary 2.4, Theorem 3.7 and §4). Put Zm = ker(T m →T m+1), use internal shifts
Q(a)j = Qa+j, and use cochain shifts K[a]j = Ka+j. At a sufficiently large cutoff N,
the BGG reciprocity theorem, applied to the regraded kernel, gives L(ZN(N)) ≃E(N).
Direct substitution in the definition of the linear complex gives
L(Q(N)) = L(Q)[−N](N).
Therefore L(ZN) ≃E[N]. For every integer m, exactness of the Tate complex gives
0 →Zm →T m →Zm+1 →0. The middle term is graded free, and its linear sheaf complex
is acyclic by the contraction above. Its associated triangle therefore gives L(Zm+1) ≃
L(Zm)[1]. Iteration yields L(ZN) ≃L(Z0)[N], hence L(Z0) ≃E with no additional twist.
The bounded complex of locally free sheaves may be split locally away from its locally
free degree-zero cohomology, so its fibres have no other homology: Z0 is proper. The
point case gives the trivial exterior module in internal degree zero. On P1, the case E =
O(−1) gives the Euler kernel of O2 →O(1), again checking the grading sign. These are
checks of conventions in the cited construction, not a new proof of the BGG reciprocity
theorem.

1.25.1
GP-1976-01: Analytic separation of the regular
representation of SL(2, R) into series
Construct invariant projectors separating the discrete and continuous parts of
L2(SL(2, R)), and realize the two discrete parts as holomorphic Hardy boundary val-
ues on explicitly specified invariant domains. This is narrower than the Plancherel
decomposition into all individual irreducibles.

published by Gelfand and Gindikin in 1977, received 6 June 1977. The notebook pro-
jector formula needs the trace-factor correction recorded in its occurrence.
1.25.2

Gelfand–Gindikin domains

Möbius contractions of the closed unit disc or its closed exterior into the correspond-
ing open region in CP1.
Occurrences: 1976-398.19.

and real-form conventions are essential.
1.25.3

ODEs
For a regular smooth scalar equation y′′ = F(x, y, y′), determine whether a local non-
singular point transformation takes the equation to Y ′′ = 0. The condition concerns
the equation on an open set, not the quadratic coefficient along only one solution.
Occurrences: 1976-403.1, 1976-403.10.
Status (2026-11):
As of 10 November 2026, classically resolved by simultaneous
vanishing of the Lie–Tresse invariants I, H. Arnold explicitly presented nineteenth-
century work; Cartan’s 1924 connection theory and the 2008/2009 modern recon-
struction verify the criterion.
The notebook’s weaker one-solution implication is
false.
1.25.4
GP-1976-04: Extension of four-dimensional supertranslations
to the Poincaré superalgebra
Adjoin Lorentz generators acting in the vector representation on translations and the
Majorana spin representation on odd generators, with the odd bracket equivariant
into translations.
Construct the resulting N = 1 Lie superalgebra without central
charges.
Occurrences: 1976-404.5.
Status (2026-11): As of 10 November 2026, resolved before the 1976 question: the
Gol’fand–Likhtman algebra circulated and was published in 1971. Likhtman’s repro-
duced preprint carries a 12 April 1971 printing date. The semidirect-product con-
struction verifies the mixed Jacobi identity through equivariance.

1.25.6
GP-1975-26: Reconstructing higher-rank commuting ordinary
differential operators
Recover a commuting algebra of scalar ordinary differential operators of generic spec-
tral rank r > 1 from correctly normalized pointed-curve, spectral-bundle and local
functional data, at least for smooth spectral curves and data in general position. A
curve and an unframed bundle alone are not sufficient input.
Occurrences: 1975-391.3, 1975-391.4, 1976-410.7, 1976-412.3.
Status (2026-11): As of November 2026, the generic smooth-curve inverse problem
is solved by the abstract Drinfeld correspondence and Krichever’s effective recon-
struction. Krichever (1978, Theorem 2.3) corrects the earlier 1977 implementation;
its received date is 15 February 1977. No claim of a complete classification of all
singular or globally regular real cases is attached to this occurrence.

For two nonsingular conics with smooth incidence elliptic curve, determine whether

Occurrences: 1976-416.1, 1976-416.2.

1.25.11
GP-1976-24: Counting Young-graph paths between
prescribed endpoints
Given µ ⊆λ, count saturated Young-graph paths from µ to λ, equivalently compute the
multiplicity of V µ in V λ|S|µ|.
Occurrences: 1976-415.6.
Status (2026-11): As of November 2026: the count is the skew-tableau number fλ/µ,
given exactly by the classical Jacobi–Trudi/Aitken determinant. The notebook states
no further asymptotic or positivity demand. The checked later derivation is Stanley
(2011, equation (1.2)).
1.25.12
GP-1976-25: Ulam asymptotics and the Plancherel first row
For a uniform permutation, or the ranks of an independent continuous sample, deter-
mine the leading asymptotic longest increasing-subsequence length.
Occurrences: 1976-415.14, 1976-415.18.
Status (2026-11): As of November 2026: Ln/(2√n) →1 in probability. This is the 1977
first-order result of Vershik and Kerov (1977, Corollaries 1–2); the separate fluctuation
discussion is not folded into this solved question.
1.25.13
GP-1976-26: Description and Torelli information of rank-one
representation packets
For a fixed marked compact Riemann surface X and degree-zero line bundle L, de-
scribe all characters with underlying holomorphic bundle L, and determine the infor-
mation about X retained by that embedded packet.
Occurrences: 1976-417.9, 1976-417.13, 1976-417.15.
Status (2026-11): As of November 2026: the packet is a coset of the exponential of
holomorphic periods; its embedded tangent space, integral marking, and intersection
pairing recover the principally polarized Jacobian and hence the curve. These are the
classical constructions and exercises in Tyurin (1978, Ch. II, §§2–3).
